FASHIONS OF TO-DAY.

I do not think the girls of the present day realise at all the importance of taking care of their clothes. Things generally are so cheap that the idea has gained ground that they are comparatively valueless, and therefore the girl who used to consider one blouse sufficient finds she cannot now exist without two or three. There are many, however, who view matters in a different and more sensible litht, and who • find that it is possible to always appear well dressed, and yet to practise economy. There are several useful points to be observed in this respect, one of the most important being to have few gowns, but to have these made at a really good place where the cut and fit are irre- proachable. The first expense will be well repaid by the satisfactory appearance of the dresses, which will wear well, and look nice even when they are shabby. The dress sketched has hit the happy medium, and evinces real good taste in all details as well as the general effect. It is made of soft fine ;lack cloth, and is trimmed with bands of

SMART DRESS.

embroidery in rich colours outlined or rather piped with red velvet. The tout ensemble is most pleasing, and the soft frou Amu of a silk underskirt and lining enhance the charm of this smart toilette. In spite of the fact that unless the silk is of a superlative quality, it proves a most unprofitable lining, the most elegant frocks are still lined thoughout with silk, the gentle rustle d Ohich having an un- doubted fascination about it with its hint of -hidden elegancies. It is a very good plan for the woman who has to consider ways and means to select the one particular colour which is most becoming to her, and to make it the key note of the whole scheme of her dres,. Among the other little golden rules which the careful minded never fail to observe are the following :—On no account put away clothes unbrushed, nor forget to put the trees in boots and shoes when they are taken off. Gloves, on being removed, should always be carefully straightened and palled into shape before they are put away, while veils should be always smoothed out and rolled up- if any reasonable amount of wear is to be got out of them. Yet another noteworthy paint is to always remove

a walking dress on returning home, as to .sit about in it will ruin its freshness.

The pretty blouse sketched owes much of its smartness to the way it is worn, as half the battle as regards the success of one's toilette lies in the •manner of putting it on. The dainty arrangement of the lace at the throat, the right adjustment of the belt, and the smoothing into place of blouse and skirt are more int- portant items than many women consider them.

The blouse (depicted is made of soft cream :elk, and has some lace applique for a trimming, and is particularly well suited for afternoon wear. There is much more in the actual way of wearing onots clothes than the ordinary English woman iraagines, indeed, more perhaps than in the clothes themselves, which if badly worn will never look well however rich and expensive the materials employed. The secrets of good dressing are as accessible to the poor as to the rich, and there is many a poor girl who appears to better advantage in her simple gown than her wealthier sister in all her elaborate toilette. There is a profound feeling of satisfaction in displaying good t,aste and a cultivated eye by adecting an admirable toilette at a small outlay.

A TURBAN TOQUE

Among the fashionable styles of bodice the crossover has once more attained a certain vogue of its own, while crossed fichus indicate the tendency of the modes. The crossover pelerine in fur is one of the newest fashions in fur, the uppermost side being ornamented by a cluster of furry tails and heads. The turban toque illustrated has a crown of soft fluffy swan:Fdown, while the brim is of sable. A brown and white aigrette caught by a jewelled clasp or brooch is fixed at the left side. This is one of the best worn styles of millinery, and looks well in conjunction with furs, or as the complzment of a severe tailor made.

There are one or two novelties in the realms of millinery, one of the newest shapes simu- lating the shcvel .hats worn by priests on the Continent The crown is round, and the brim oval back and front.

These are to be seen in velvet or beaver, and have the otherwise somewhat trying lines of the brim softened by a big ostrich feather, which, sweeping from the front, falls on the hair behind in a graceful manner. It is not, however, given to every wcman to attempt to wear such a hat successfully, as a particularly well-dressed coiffure and a good deal of style are necessary qualifications. Another novelty as regards shape has the crown made high at the back, and decreasing in height towards the front. The trimming for thia style is also one or two ostrich feathers,

WOMEN'S CHAT.

The Drawing Room, as it is now held at Dublin Castle, and of which the first of the season took place this week, is identical in form to the Courts at Bucking- ham Palace. Formerly it was the privilege of the Lord Lieutenant to kiss the cheeks of debutantes on their presentation but now he merely bows. The Mite before Lord and Lady Dudley takes place in the throne room, about ten o'clock, afterwards refreshments are served at side tables, but there is no formal supper, nor is there on similar occasions at Buckingham Palace. Now that the University of Dublin has announced its intention to confer degrees upon women, it is to be hoped that Oxford and Cambridge will shortly follow this example. It is hard to allow women to qualify for the examinations, and, when they have passed, to deny them the titles which would be theirs, if they were men. A similar distinction is made in the fellowship of several learned societies, but it is significant that the most distinguished of these—the Linnean Society —will shortly admit women to its meetings, and the privileges of full fellowship. Princess Louise (Duchess of Argyll) is taking an active part in the efforts that are being made to establish an institution for the children of Scottish soldiers and sailors. For this purpose £50,000 is required, and, as it is intended that the institution should be a Scottish memorial to Queen Victoria, there should be a liberal response to the appeal for funds which has been issued by Her Royal Highness. At the same time it is not a little discouraging that funds are still wanting to carry out the memorials of the late Queen Victoria in the capital of the empire. But the project in which Princess Louise, the Earl of Rosebery, and others are interested, has a very useful, as well as a sentimental purpose in view, and if the institution is founded, the government will make its maintenance a public charge, as is already the case with the Royal Hibernian Schools, and the Duke of York's Military School in London. Haddon Hall, the seat of the venerable Duke of Rutland, has once before been visited by their present Majesties. It is one of the most famous of historical mansions, and was the scene of the romance of Dorothy Vernon, who made a runaway match with the son of the first Earl of Rutland, whom she believed at the time to be only a forrester. Another great point of interest in Haddon Hall is that it has practically remained un- altered since medimval times. The banqueting ball is seven hundred years old, and is the same now as it was in the time of Henry VII. There is also a mirror which is said to have reflected the features of Queen Elizabeth and there are many wonderful tapestries, the late Duchess of Rutland having devoted much of her time to their preservation and renovation. Thanks to the Duke, visitors and tourists are freely permitted to view the treasures and the architectural beauties of Haddon Hall.

Several of the largest and best carpets from Sand- ringham House have been destroyed by a fire at Kings Lynn, where they had been sent to be cleaned. The loss is, no doubt, a heavy one, but it would have been still more serious had they been some of the priceless carpets at Windsor Castle. They are mostly of Eastern manu- facture, but also include some valuable specimens of home made carpets. One of the best of the old English carpets in existence is in possession of Sir Tatton Sykes at Slidmere. The factory where it was made at Wens- ford in Yorkshire has long since disappeared,but the coloring in the carpet is as beautiful and as fresh as it was a century ago, and it is very large, being no less than 120 feet long, and 40 feet wide. Those women whose dress allowance is small, and whose ambitions in the direction of cut and style are somewhat above their means, should takeadvantage of the present slack season to obtain a well-cut well-built tailor gown at moderate price. All the leading tailors and dressmakers take orders on greatlyredaced terms at this season of the year. The new modes are not yet declared, and will not be for the next few weeks to come,when the rush for spring toilettes will commence. Meanwhile large staffs of workers have to be kept on in readiness,and firms prefer, during this period of wait- ing, to accept lower terms rather than none at all. The woman who decides upon a plain skirt, fluted about the feet, and well-fitting about the hips' with box pleats, or single pleats stitched flat at the back, and tightly fitting coat, ending four or five inches below the waist, and which can be worn open or closed, the sleeves showing no exaggeration, may rest assured that she will be well dressed, and quite sufficiently up-to- date for another year. Stitching should be the only forth of trimming, strappings being too popular. All shades of grey will be very modish this coming spring and summer, and so far as fabrics are concerned, serges, and face cloths, will prove quite safe selections. Modistes are returning from Delhi with endless " ideas " and we shall probably find touches of the ‘" gorgeous East" about the majority of spring and summer toilettes. The Durbar plumehasalreadyarrived, and is appearing in many up-to-date hats and toques. This so-called plume, is a very tall tuft of thin flat bristles, dyed in strips of vivid colours,andcrmm.—The boss, from which it springs, is usually jewelled. The reign of the Durlxir plume promises to be, among " exclusive " dressers, a short one. Cheap imitations are already being offered, and word. Petal work is a new system of decorating cards, by which some beautiful colour effects may be produced without any special skill being required. The petal paints, which can be had from chemists are supplied together with instructions, in boxes at from Is. lid. to 3s. each, and those with artistic tastes will find them an easy means of producing the most exquisite effects in colour, shading and designs. Few women attach so much importance to brushing their teeth as they do to brushing their hair. It is one of the minor operations of the toilet, and very often not so much importance is attached' to it as ought to be. How many for instance, brush the upper teeth from the gums downwards, and the lower teeth from the gums upwards. The lusual custom is to use the brush horizontally, but this tends to make the gums recede, whereas the upward and downward brushing tends to draw the gums more over the teeth, and this greatly helps to preserve them.. The appeal on behalf of the famine-stricken fisher folk in Brittany has evoked many expressions of sympathy, and we ought in this country to remember the generous self-sacrifice of the Briton women and girls at the time of the loss of the " Drummond Castle." This was no less kindly because no doubt it was partly due to the reverence which all French people manifest for the dead. In this regard our own people may learn a lesson from their • neighbours, who, when they visit the metropolis, and the majority of provincial towns, must regard with amazement the common spectacle of men permitting a funeral to pass without raising their hats. A pretty and delicious sweet is made as follows: Peel, core and quarter some apples, stew them ina little water. Sweeten and beat to a pulp. Stir in a little dissolved gelatine, put the mixture into a wetted mould. When set and cold turn into a glass dish, pour round a

HOCKEY.

LATCHFORD _V. STOC KT ON. HEATH

These teams met at Stockton Heath on a fast ground. From the bully off the game was exceedingly fast and interesting, but at half-time neither side could claim the advantage, nothing being scored. From the restart both sides attacked in turn, but the defence on both sides was good, and it was some time before the visitors scored from a neat centre by Spensley, which Albinson converted. From this point each team played with renewed vigour, but Latchford were not allowed to keep the lead long, as Hatton soon equalized for the homesters. Elated with this success the home team again attacked strongly, but the defence was good, and their efforts were repulsed by judicious hitting by the visiting backs, Carter being especially prominent. Both teams fought hard for another point, ancl eventu- ally Latchford were successful, Albinson scoring a second goal after a slight mistake by the home back. This reverse aroused the home team to further efforts, and they had hard lines in not again drawing level, as Owen netted the ball, but was ruled off-side. Nothing further was scored, so Latchford ran out winners of a hard-fought but pleasant game by two goals to one. The back division of each team played a sound game ' except for one or two slight mistakes, but, for the visitors, Knight, at right half, was worthy of special note, playing a brilliant game throughout.

LATCHFORD 2ND V. STOCKTON HEATH. Played at Latchford, and resulted in a win for the visitors. Play ranged from end to end, but at half-time the visitors led by one goal to niL During the second half the home team played a better game, and Grounds equalized by a good shot. They were unable to keep level long and Fairclough scored a second goal for the visitors. Final score—Stockton Heath, two goals; Latchford, one.

DAMP BEDS AND DISEASE Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Rheu- matism and Chest Complaints.

An ill aired bed has led many a man tot his early grave. The chill of the sheets, drawing all the heat out of the body, sets the muscles and joints of the rheumatic sufferer aching; but worse still, the cold and damp sap the vitality of those whose lungs are not strong; a cough sets in, the inflamed blood vessels give way, the patient spits blood after a week or two—and he has no blood to spare. Then come wasting and the nightly perspiration which accompanies and aggravates lost strength; and we all know what the end is. Con- sumption means Death if not early dealt with. At the first sign of a Cough, at the first twinge of Rheumatism a wise man will look to his health. Treat the trouble rationally. The greatest safeguard against illness is an abundance of good blood. That is why purgatives are so fatal a folly. They waste and im- poverish blood just when new blood is needed. A sick man or a weakly woman should avoid all medicines which move the bowels, as they would avoid poison. They do no good; all the good that any purgative can do, Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People will do far better and more permanently. They cure Indigestion and Bile by the natural, rational method, building up the system, expelling Rheumatism, healing irritated blood vessels, saving impaired lungs. A typical instance is that of Mr. George Grove Boswell, market gardener, Goldsithney, St. Michael's Mount, Cornwall, reprinted from The Cornishman. He said:

"In October, 1901. I slept in a damp bed and very soon felt ill effects. My Heart and Lungs became affected and I got weaker and weaker until I could hardly walk. I suffered also from Shortness of Breath. I became so weak that I could only get upstairs by pulling =self up the bannisters. "Happily, I was persuaded to give Dr. Williams' Pink Pills a trial. The first doses gave me instant relief, and as I continued to take them for some time my strength gradually returned, and my breathing wonderfully im- proved. lam quite well now and wonderfully strong for a man of 71. "I consider every single one of Dr. Williams' Pink Pills I have taken,has been worth a sovereign to me. Since I have taken them I have not felt the slightest touch of Rheumatism,though preciously I was racked by it continually. I am sure I should have been a dead man long before this had I not taken these pills; they certainly saved my life, and I have told all my neigh- bours so." Liniments and Embrocations will not cure Rheuma- tism; neither will purgatives: they cannot drive the poison from the blood: you might as well expect to cure Anwmia with them, or Kidney Disease. All these things Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People are con- stantly curing, as well as Consumption, Bronchitis, Eczema, Fits, Gout, Heart Disease, Paralysis, and the ailments which women suffer in silence. Only—it is the genuine pills, not the substitutes which' some shopmen push, that cure. Substitutes cure nothing. Dr. Williams' name on the pink wrapper is a guarantee of genuineness: any pink pills that don't bear Dr. Williams' name are not Dr. Williams' Pills. Refuse them. Go to an honest shop, where they sell you what you ask for without parley,qr send direct for the ptlls to Dr. Williams' Medicine Company, Holborn Viaduct, London, enclosing the price, 2s. 9d. for a box, 12s. 9d. for six boxes, post free.

Warrington P.S. A. Mr. Clegg presided at the weekly meeting of this Society on Sunday. Mr. Hannon sang two solos in good style, being accompanied on the piano by Mr. Wadsworth. Mr. Wood • (vice-president) .was the spekear, and he gave an address on the motto for the year " Advance." Cheshire Agricultural Show.

A. meeting of the members of the Cheshire Agricul- tural Society was held at Crewe on Monday, Mr. A. R. Briggs (Congleton) presiding. The Secretary reported that the Warrington Agricultural Society had altered the date of their show so as not to clash with .the Cheshire show fixed to be held at Nantwich on August 6. song discussion followed, it being pointed out that this date would clash with the Yorkshire show. —Mr. C. E. Parton (Haughton Hall) proposed that the date of the Cheshire Show at .Nantwich be fixed for August 27.. Mr. Sadler seconded and Mr. J, Beecroft (Chester) supported the proposition. The motion was carried by 19 votes to 11.

Girls' Social. About sixty members of the Girls' Club, Towns End, had an enjoyable social the other evening. An excellent tea was served, and afterwards the chair was taken by Mrs. Garnett, who spoke very helpfully to the members, and then- announced an attractive pro- gramme. Songs were ably rendered by the Misses Wilson and Collin, and some sacred duets by two members of the club. Mrs. Davies, the kindly and clever matron, contributed a capital Lancashire read- ing, and Miss M. Hampson aroused the closest atten- tion and interest by her recitation. But the gramo- phone, kindly manipulated by Mr. Johnson, secured the lion's share of the enthusiasm of the audience, and caused great amusement and delight. Miss B. Dutton proposed a vote of thanks to the hon. secretary, Miss Ashdown, and to Mrs. Davies for their constant labours on behalf of the club, which is open four evenings of the week and is extremely useful to a large number of girls who have few or no advantages. Votes were also warmly passed to those who had so 'kindly entertained, or contributed to the tea.

The Examiner, Friday, February 6, 1903.

OUGII rffl L

\To-E. jy

G AT E.

AMY MILLER,

Author of "The Jewelled Dagger," "A Froll Shadow,' "The Romance of a Rack "Haunted Lives,

&c.

I stood, traiksfixed by an ovnwhelming horror, scarcely able to realise that the sight before tho was not the creation of my (mil brain. It could not be true Only two hours ago I hai left him—this friend of my bosom, this man I loved and trusted above all other men. -Only two hours ago there had been laughter on his lips and in his kindly eyes. We had parted with a jest and now, although I looked upon his face, although I held his hand in mine, Anstruther and I would meet no more on earth.

I had found him fallen forward upon the table at which he had been seated, writing. He had been stabbed to death!

There was a door behind him leading from the corridor. The assassin had had but a few paces to tread from the door to the victim, and the floor was so thickly carpeted that it was easy to enter unheard.

But who had reason to compass the death of Anstruther ? The motive had certainly not been robbery, as very quickly transpired. We had come that morning, he and I, strangers to the great hotel, where men and women of all countries Caine and went. Impossible to gx suspicion upon any one of the many whose temporary dwelling was the same as our own.

For a year past we had been comrades, Anstruther and I. He was my senior by some seven years at least; but this did not detract from our close friendship. I, who was but twenty-one, an orphan, with rib near relatives, and who had never met a wonian who had called forth anything but the most transient passion in my heart, loved Anstruther with all the strength of my being. . That he should regard nie with like feelings I neither hoped nor expected; but I knew that his affection for me was strong and true, and the past year had been the happiest, of my life. Now all was done, and I was alone and desolate. He was gone from me, my hero and my friend, stricken down in the pride of his manhood, snatched from me by the hand of an unsuspected, yet most deadly foe. So far as I knew he had not an enemy in the world. Such as profited by his death were beyond suspicion. What had he done, whose beauty and fascination won him friends wherever he went, to deserve so cruel a fate?

All efforts to solve the mystery were futile. The chief of the police even hinted that he believed an addition was made to the already long list of undiscovered crimes. I lingered on in Paris, following now one clue, now another, failing always to throw the desired light upon the baffling darkness.

But by my friend's dead body I had registered a vow. Rash and foolish, perchance, but wrung from a heart almost broken. Never to enjoy the rich inheritance now mine, never to make a dwelling- place of my ancestral home, never to seek the lovo of woman till Gervys Anstruther was avenged

Two years had passed away, and the dark shadow which had fallen, over my life upon that terrible day when I found Anstruther dead, oppressed me still.

There were times, it is true, when it seemed that the intensity of my grief was passing away; for I was young, and might have tasted to the full all the pleasures of life. But then the recollection of my friend's dead face would rise before me, and something that was like remorse would fill my bosom because I had been willing to forget for a while his cruel fate and the task so clearly assigned me.

I was in London, whither I had gone in the following up of a supposed clue, whicb led, as all the rest had done, to nothing, when I met an old college chum, George Irving. It was three or four years since we had seen each other, though we had been close companions once, and, at his earnest entreaty, I went to spend a few days with him at his home.

It was -a country house, about an hour's journey by rail from London. Thereswere to be no other guests, and the family, I knew, consisted of Mr. and Mrs. Irving and George's two sisters. The brief journey down was a very pleasant one. My companion's bright, unclouded face, his ready laugh and gay spirits transported me to the glad world where we had once lived together.. My own youth awoke in sympathy. Surely for a while I might put by my gloomy memories and my self- appointed work of justice ? I cannot tell how it was that, as we left the little station and drove in the light dog-cart that awaited us along the country road, the recollection of my vow came upon Inc with overwhelming force. I could think of nothing but the tragic scenes of two years ago. It was a September evening. The scent of autumn was in the air, and the lurid light of an angry sunset shed its glow upon the woods through which our road lay.

"We shall soon be at home now," said George's cheery voice. "The Priory lies in a hollow, you know, and so we shall not see it till we are close Upon it. It's the jolliest old place in the world, Bertram. I've always wanted you to see it." He spoke with happy pride of his home. And my home had no master. I thought of the eager welcome awaiting him. Ald happy George I was watching him, half-wistfully. His eyes were glancing about at every well-remembered and well-loved landmark. His lips wore a smile of pleased anticipation. Suddenly I saw a change, a flicker of emotion, ardent, and gladly surprised. Amidst the trees by the roadside a woman Stood, and while I wondered at her beauty I was tormented by a vague unrest and one of those half- memories we have all known. I could not tell of what she reminded me, but I knew it .was of something painful and pitiable. From behind the trees shone the nvestern sky, all aflame vth rose and amber. The red beams lit the crimson and yellow leaves . that yet clothed the branches thickly, and the glory of the dying day and the sad splendour of the waning year were in harmony with her who stood before us.

Sie was young, graceful and tall; and to features, beautiful in form, were given those rich and glowing lints beloved of the old Venetian painters. Her head was uncovered, and her hair shone like ruddy gold. She held a mass of trailing, crimson vine-leaves in her arms. George drew up his horse and bent_ to speak to her. Then a child ran out from amidst the trees with a shout of welcome. She was George's younger sister, a little girl of seven, pretty, win- some, but decidedly spoilt. The lady was intro- duced to me as Miss Salviati.

"I thought you had no visitors, George," I remarked as ive drove on again.

"Nor have we," he replied. "Miss Salviati is our 'constant guest.. Silo oaine here, actually, to be Nettle's governess.. is like one of ourselves now."

"She is 'singularly beautiful," I said, Musingly, still haunted by hiperfect recollection.' George glanced at me quickly. 1 frankly returned his look, guessing his thought. Already I.had learnt two things—George was madly in love, and the object of his passion would never return it. I was not likely to be his rival even in wish, and this I think he understood. No, her Irauty did not touch that secret spring in my heart which is in the heart of every man, and often so mysteriously moved. He did not even assent to my praise, hut looked away before him. The shadows were fallirg fait. His young face was troubled by the reflection of unutterable emotions.

"Your sisters are very fond of her, no doubt ?" I said at last. "Npttie adores her," he answered.., turning to me again. "And Mildred—oh, Mildred loves her dearly, also, of course, only being older she is less demonstrative in her affection." I detected a note of uneasiness here, as though he tried to convince himself of what he was forced to doubt.

"How terrible it seems," he went on, "that she, with the blood of nobles in her veins, should be compelled to earn her bread." "She is not English ?" I queried. - "No ; she is a Venetian. She is the last of an ancient., but impoverished, family." "At least she is fortunate in having found such a happy home and such kind friends. "Giacinta Salviati would find friends anywhere." he responded, with a touch -of pride in the woman he would never win almost pathetic. "But I think she is content with us. She is devoted to tri, mother and Nettie; and Mildred, beinv only a few years younger than herself, is naturally her friend: and—and lain sure she loves the old place, too. Look- Bertram.'t here it is!"

He pointed with his whip to the old grey mansion down in the hollow. I knew he pictured Giacints its mistress. We entered the carriacce drive and were soon alighting at the broad flight of steps that led to the wide door. .

It was hospitably open. A flood of cheerful home-light poured from thence. A gentleman with silvery hair and upright figure was awaiting us, and beside him a young girl dr,ssed in white.

Just a slender, %-'ve'et-faced girl of seventeen, with soft, light-brown Itar and lustrous grey eyes, and a dainty, delicate .bloom on her rounded cheeks. But I looked at her, and I forgot the gorgeous vision of Giacinta in the autumn wood. I forgot poor George and his hopeless love. I forgot Anstruther, my Tow, myself—all but her ! One touch of those small soft fingers, one glance of those sweet eyes, and the secret spring was moved ; the doer of my heart flew open wide, and Mildred Irving entered in, to reign its queen for evermore !

The week, at first proposed for my stay, passed away all too swiftly, and my visit was lengthened for a few days more.

Days stolen from Paradise for me, though the dark hours of the night brought ever the recollec- tion of my vow.

I had not broken it. By no word or look had I revealed my love, though Mildred and I were much together. One strong., kindred taste we had, in addition to many subtler affinities—this was the love of Art.

I could only appreciate, for creative talent I bad none ; but Mildred had unusual gifts, and, having now left school, devoted, a great deal of time to their cultivation. A room on the, ground floor, with long windows opening on the garden, was het studio and favourite retreat. George and I were often there ; but Giacinta, I observed, seldom entered it.

Between her and Mildred there was a barrier, invisible, but impassable. The younger girl gave to the other, invariably, all the delicate attention and unobtrusive kindness that real friendship itself could have prompted, but I saw she did not love her • while Giacinta, on her part, appeared almost to shrink from any attempt at the affectionate intimacy that might have been expected to exist between two young women, equals in birth and education, if not in wealth, living together in a country house. Giacinta, however, was capable of strong attach- ment. She was devoted to gentle Mrs. Irving. She lavished upon little Nettie almost impassioned love. 1 met her one evening, when the two had been absent a long while, coming throu4,rh the glades of the park with the child asleep in hex arms. She would not let me relieve her of her burden, though her step told that she was. weary ; but • she went on, with the little figure in her embrace, and upon her face, and in her whole bearing that ineffable tenderness that comes to a woman only when she looks upon a child. Ah, she was meant for love, she was meant for maternity, this beautiful and sorrowful creature ! How was it I could never picture her the centre of a peaceful home? It was that same night that I awoke from a troubled dream that of late had visited me often.

In proportion as my waking hours, spent in the society of Mildred, were happy, the dark nights were filled with sorrow.

In My dreams, I was constantly with Gervys A.nstrut her. Again and again I visited the room where I.had found him 'dead ; and, with peculiar vividness and distinctness, every detail of that fatal room, down to its most trifling, ornament or chance disorder, was brought before me. It seemed that Mildred was always at my side. I had never talked to her of Anstruther. George may have told her of the tragedy, but he had no idea of the manner in which it had coloured my whole existence.

She was so bright, so Mitonched by any shadow of trouble, my pretty Mildred! I would not sadden her serene spirit by dwelling on the dark history in which I had borne a part. Yet I longed as never before to penetrate the mystery, for my vow stood between me and my soul's desire.

I awoke, as 1 have said, from that oft-recurring dream. I was suddenly wide awake, restless, and full of a strange expectancy. Sleef. had become an impossibility, for the difficulties in which I was involved came upon me in the exaggerated form all trials and forebodings wear in the night. I rose, and, partly dressed, determined to drown my thoughts by reading, since "beyond the ivory gate " was nothing but disquiet for me. Mystical region w hence Anstruther's image came, and where Mildred's gentle spirit met my own! Between George's room and mine was a smaller one, half dressing-room, half sitting-room. I had left a book there. T would go and get it. There was no communicating door. I stepped out on the gallery, which ran round three sides of the great hall, and to which the principal rooms opened. I had no light in my hand, for the moonlight, streaming through the tall windows, gave sufficient light for me, and I knew where my book was.

The opposite side of the gallery was in shadow. I stood for some moments admiring the picturesque effect of the silver moonshine on the old carved staircase, the banners and suits of mail. No wonder George was proud of his ancestral home. One could picture a lady in dim brocade pacing the gallery, and glidinc, down the staircase—the ghost of some fair frying dead and gone. Or, perchance, a monk of even more distant days, revisiting the dwelling-place of his brotherhood. Was it fancy? Surely something moved there on the staircase, amidst the silver patches of light and the dense shadows cast by the oaken carving. A 'veritable White Lady was gliding, step by step, to the hall below, and there the shadows swallowed her sup. In some part recovering from ray surprise and ' eerie sensations I hurried to the staircase and down to the hall. But the white figure had completely vanished, and I returned to my • room mystified. After all, I reasoned, I might have been deceived. Some effect of the vivid moonlight, my own excited and disturbed imagination, had called up the ghost between them. But I took an opportunity next day, when I Sat in the studio with George and Mildred, of turning the conversation on ghosts. The Priory claimed more than one. Mildred and George, in turn, related family legends. I kept my nocturnal experience to myself, however; and from ghosts we went on to supernatural warnings, prophetic dreams, and the like. George laughed at it all, and professed uncom- promising scepticism ; Mildred spoke as if, like myself, she believed

"There are more things in Heaven and earth Than are dreamt of in our philosophy." . .

She possessed a most impressiOnable and sensitive nature. Her instincts and perceptions were reritarkably keen. Often, even in those early days, I have seen the comprehension of some thought of mine lighting up her large eyes before my lips had uttered it.

I felt that it was impossible for her not to know how dearly I loved her. I was sure she did know it, and I had sometimes hoped, in spite of all, that Mildred loved me.

George sauntered away presently through the open French window, which was, I need hardly say, an innovation. I had seen Giacinta at a little distance, and knew he had gone to join her. "Mr. Arundel,". said Mildred, gently, "I am going to tell you something. George would laugh at. me, but you will understand, I know.. lam very troubled." She looked up at me with the clear, confiding gaze of a child. Perplexity, fear - and awe were in the depths of those dear eyes lifted to mine. "When we talked of ghosts just now," she went on, almost in a whisper, "1 could nbt feel as George does. Mr. Arundel, this room is haunted:. Every night it is visited, and I have discovered this in so strange a manner--"

. She was trelilling and very pale. 'What would I not have given to clasp her in my arms and kiss her quivering lips. "fell me all, dear Miss Irving," I entreated. "Be assured there is some natural explanation." "If you locked a room fast at night, carried away the k,), with you, hid it in a place known only to yourself, and then found the room had been entered, would you seek a natural explanation ? " "I think Ishould. 'Mow do you know the room is entered? "

"A week ago I found, On a canvas left upon an easel ready for use next day, the outlines of _a pieti.re. I was frightened. • I cannot quite explain why, except that the picture was a tragic one. spoke of it to no one. You -know, the servants have orders never to interfere with my palettes, canvases, and -ketelvs ; never, till that night, locked l.- is door. Next morning I found the easel as I had lei it, 1.11,111e1 away into a corner and covered with a dm!.r But I lifted up the covering, and I found,"— shuddered—" the mysterioEs ortiet had been at work again !" -

"The wl ndows," I suggested. "They are fastened securely upon the inside. There is but One door. You inuuine .someone .i.s

playing me a trick, perhaps r No ctict i at firm That is why I kept silence, and took such care to fasten up the room from all intruders. Still, as I tell you, the work goes on, the picture grows to completion."

"-In old houses like this, there are often sliding panels and secret doors."

- "Oh, there are nono here, I am convinced. This part of the house has been so completely renovated, it must have been discovered. Only recently papa had the electric light fitted. Besides, Mr. Arundel, I do not think anyone in the house could paint such n picture as this. It is terrible, but it ie wonderful '

"May I gee it ?" "Yes," sho paling but evidently glad to share her weird secret,

A painter ghost was a novelty to me. Had my White Lady any connection with the mystery? waited with interest and curiosity, whilst Mildred drew a curtain and lifted a drapery from an easel. How shall I express my horrified amazement? The shock of grief and misery I had felt two years before was upon me again. I saw upon the canvas, pourtrayed with such realism that I seemed to be viewing actualities, the scene of Anstruther's death. The actual blow was being struck. He, with a last effort, had half- risen and beheld his murderer. His face was turned towards us.

But the figure behind him was but an undefined shadow, an impalpable terror, without face or form.

I staggered tack from the easel, linable to conceal my emotion. Mildred covered the picture from sight. "I must destray it!" she said. "It fills me with dread. It is not like a picture; it is too real." "Miss Irving," I asked, trying to speak in my usual voice, and failing, "will you grant me a request ?"

"Oh, yes," she responded. "Will you let me watch here to-night, unknown to anyone?" ___

"I.' had rather you did not," she answered, artlessly. "I should be afraid for you." "There is no need. Do you give me leave to come here, when all the household are at rest? I shall solve the mystery, lam certain. It is not only that I cannot bear you to suffer any further uneasiness, I have auother reason which I will one day explain." "One that concerns yourself ?" she asked, quickly.

_ . "Yes, and all my life's happiness." "Then come here, if you will," she said; "but I wish the night were over!" "The picture is not yet complete, you see. The figure of the murderer has yet to appear. And now let us forget this till to-morrow morning, when you shall hear my adventyres." I do not suppose she forgot it any more than I did—and I know I thought of it all day long, and that it filled my soul with alternate hope and dread as night fell. Should I find what I had sought so long in vain, here, where I had never sought it ? Would the picture be finished that night, and in my presence? I was pacing the garden walk, in the light of the newly-risen moon, and presently my reverie was broken in upon by the sound of voices behind a hedge of yews: George's voice, in earnest pleading; Giacinta Salviati's--soft, melodious, but, ab, how sad!

I moved away; but soon 'after she came down the alley where I walked. She wa9 alone; George had left her; and she turned, without seeing me, to look after his retreating figure. There was a white drapery about her head. I saw that she had been weeping; and, looking at her thus, I knew of what she had always reminded me. Do you know that portrait of Beatrice Cenci which hangs in the Barberini Gallery ? If so, you will at once recall that face, so exquisitely fair, those locks of richest auburn, the dark eyes, full of unfathomable woe, in such pathetic contrast to lips of almost childlike beauty. Giacinta Salviati was like that picture! When all the house was silent, I stole out from my room, but before making my way to the studio, I waited awhile to see if my White Lady walked each night. I did not wait, in vain. Presently I saw the white shape gliding on the oaken stair. This time,l was determined ,she should not elude me. I followed her into the hall, down a corridor. She appeared to be going before me to the studio. In the winding passages, I lost her; but I found her again,_ _ _ _

-Mildred had promised to leave the key of the studio door in the lock. The door was ajar. There was a light within. Did the spirit need the electric light, then, for its work? I entered noiselessly, breathlessly. There, before the easel, sat a figure in white robes and streaming hair, at work upon the dread painting—my White Lady. It was Mildred herself!

I moved cautiously forward, so that I saw her face. She was quite unconscious of my presence— asleep, or, rather, in a trance. Her large eyes were open wide and fixed upon her work. dared not wake her. Silent and awestricken, I watched and waited, while under the hand of my. innocent love the likeness of Anstruther's destroyer was made manifest to me! When it was finished, she rose, put the easel in its former position, turned off the light, and passed, with the same gliding step, along the passage, through the corridor and hall, and up the staircase. I saw her enter her own room, and returned to my own, far more disturbed than when I quitted it. Through the ivory gate, whence come spirits to the souls of men, had come the awful vision to my darling. I could not believe that with Anstruther and all his surroundings so faithfully pourtrayed, the figure added that night could be other than the murderer. •

What, then, must be done? I Mildred was before me in the studio. I am sure my haggard face prepared her for something strange; but my relation of what I had actually seen bewildered her. 4, . "It cannot be, Mr. Arundel !" she exclaimed. "I cannot paint like that.; And, besides, how could I do it in my sleep ? " _

. "Look at he wliite dressing-gown you wore last night," I answered. "You will find splashes of paint where the brush fell from your hand. Have you looked at the picture ? " - "No, I have npt yet had courage, though I have brought the easel forward, as you see." I would have prepared her, but with a sudden •gesture she flung asicb the covering. A cry burst from her lips, and she turned shuddering away. I drew her into my arms, and hiding her face on my shoulder she gave way to frightened tears.

For the face and the figure added last night to the picture were those of Giacintz Saloiati !

'Bien, while I endeavoured to soothe her we were both startled by a noise in the grounds out- side, the clatter of hoofs, shouts and cries. • • I rushed to the window and out upon the lawn. Mildred followed me. Along the carriage-drive came a pony belonging to George. It was tearing madly along, and Nettie —who, I afterwards learnt, had mounted it in the stable-yard in spite of the entreaties of the lad who had charge of it—was clinging to its mane and shrieking with fright. While I ran With all speed to the rescue I saw Giacinta rush out from amidst the trees, catch the pony by the bridle, and hold it fast while it plunged and kicked. The harm was done before I could reach het. Mischievous Nettie was safe and sound, 'and nG more frightened than she deserved to be. But Giacinta-- She was carried within to the, studio, which was the first place come. to. A doctor was sett' for, and came presently upon the scene. But she was beyond human aid. She had given her life to save Nettie from harm. While she lay there, with Mrs; Irvinc,'s hand in hers, and poor George, for whom she had no thought, watching - her from the background with yearning eyes, someone passing by the easel, which Mildred had covered, disturbed the drapery and let it fall. The dying girl gazed with horror, upon the

picture. "Oh, my sin, my sin!" she moaned. ‘lio,r comes it to rise before me now!"

There is no need to tell at length that painful story. It is enough for me to say that Gervys Anstruther was a villain, and Giacinta a deeply wronged and injured woman. She had avenged. herself. She had suffered and repented. And she gave her life for an innocent child. • Seven years have passed since then. Mildred and I have spent them together. George has never married, but there is a fair-haired, blue-eyed girl who loves him fondly, and we have hopes that ho will yet be happy. [THE END. I

AN IMMENSE SHOW OF MOTOR CARS.

The motor car show which has been suck a success during the past week at the Crystal Palace is the largest of the kind that has ever been held outside Paris. The exhibitors number over 200, and the amount of floor space which is occupied by their stands amounts to over 87,000 square feet, with a frontage of over four miles. For the most part the exhibitors are British, but a number of Leading Continental makes of motor vehicle are represented through English agents. Every type of automobile is on view, from the steam-driven heavy goods wagon to. the motor bicycle. The petrol vehicle, of course, shows an overwhelming preponderance over the steam and electrical varieties, a state of things which appears to be destined to be continued indefinitely, owing to its superior effi- ciency, economy, and simplicity. A car that has n aurally attract considerable amount of atten- tion is the new 22 h.-p. vehicle which has been built by the Daimler Motor Company for tne King, being the fourth car which they have built to the order of His Majesty. The new car is intended for shooting and estate purposes, and will be princi- pally used by loaders. It has been fitted with a canopy top, which will carry a certain amount of luggage, while cartridge boxes are placed beneath the seats.

I Martyred King," in Trafalgar Square, with wreaths and bouquets bearing inscriptions, which would not be tolerated but for their ineptitude. This year the police took the matter quietly but firmly in hand ; and, while allowing the usual decoration of the statute strictly under their super- vision, they removed anything they considered objectionable or subversive to law and order to the satisfaction of all, save the Legitimists themselves.

Thus this annual tradition becomes a pleasant function and ceases to show reprehensible features: and it is to be hoped that this judicious action will help to tone down the absurd exaggerations of the small clique, who proudly call themselves Jacobites. On many occasions they have gone too far, notabky on the night of the late Queen's death, when they published proclamations upholding their tenets and questioning King Edward's claims. Their preten- sions are too futile to be taken seriously : but, at the same time, they stood in need of a gentle cheek. /

THE DOOM OF THE ORWELL.

The fact that a destroyer can be cut in two by a cruiser throws yet another unpleasant sidelight on the British Navy. While manoeuvring round the lights opposite Corfu on Saturday night, the cruiser Pioneer collided with the destroyer Orwell, and completely severed the latter in twain. The fore part of the Orwell sank, and the stern was towed into port by the Pioneer. Several lives are known to have been lost, and many men are still missing. The Orwell formed part of a squadron of twelve destroyers, which were in pursuit of the Pioneer, a third-class cruiser of 2,200 tons. The Orwell was a twin-screw torpedo-boat destroyer of 360 tons, built by Laird Bros., Birkenhead, in 1898. She was com- manded by Lieut. Percy A. Roberts. New acci- dents to destroyers, occurring as they do in such

THE DESTROYER ORWELL, WHICH WAS CUT IN TWO BY THE PIONEER ON

SATURDAY SIGHT.

rapid sequence, arouses serious questioning. If these match-box war vessels are so easily broken up in time of peace, it is asked, what will be their use in the grim, destructive reality of war? If they can be lost so easily in peace by misadventure, what will be their chance of survival when every effort is directed towards their destruction? The answer made to this by naval experts is that the number of accidents is due to the constant manceuvring that takes place, and that even at thp cost of life and money it is better to have accidents than to dispense with the manceuvring. The comparative immunity of foreign powers from similar accidents is put down to the imperfection of their system of manceuvring. It is claimed that in war time the conditions would indubitably be reversed.

REFORM IN THE NAVY.

The rumours during the past week that for some time the Lords of the Admiralty and the heads of departments have been divided into two parties has excited much public interest. The one party, it is said, is anxious for reform in various important matters, the other—which is composed of strongly conservative veterans—is perfectly satisfied with the present condition of affairs. Admiral Sir John Fisher, the second Sea Lord, who has lately returned from the Mediterranean, and is universally regarded as the ablest officer in the Navy, heads the progressives, and Lord Walter Kerr is the mainstay of the opposition party. After long discussion the reform recently published which recognised the immense importance of the engineers in modern navies was brought about. Then came a pause. The progressives began to talk of other things. The disastrous neglect of our naval gunnery was referred to; but the veterans stiffened their backs and hung on. This rapid progres they found was abso- lutely terrifying.

Mr. -A r n ow 1 a_ Forster was put up in the House

to rebut Mr. Leicester Harms- WHO WILL RESIGN HIS POSITION worth's search- AT THE ADMIRALTY AND TAKE ing questions on THE PORTSMOUTH COMMAND, naval gunnery. • Many of th• Saval Secretary's statements have since been :proved to be based on misinformation, and more will yet be heard of it in Parliament. Yet his denials were sufficient to check popular feeling for the time, and the party of non-reform at the Admiralty seemed likely to triumph. But they had to reckon with men as able as Sir John Fisher and as enthusiastic as Prince Louis of Battenberg, the Director of Naval Intelligence, and one of the keenest of our naval officers. Further efforts were made by these progressives, but to no effect. And now it is practically settled that Sir John Fisher shall leave the Admiralty altogether. He will resign his position in Whitehall in order to take up the command at Portsmouth. The loss to the Admiralty will be irreparable, though, indeed, Sir John will have a splendid opportunity for reform and reorganisation in his new position. From the national point of view, however, the fact that he has been overruled by the non-reform party is most lamentable.

SIR JOHN FISHER,

BRITAIN'S BIGGEST BOAT.

The Cedric which has just been finished at Belfast, is the largest boat of any description ever turned out of an English dockyard. She is a White Star liner, a nine-decker, 709 feet long, or 6.?, feet shorter than the Kaiser 11711,ei,a /1., which is Germany's biggest 1,..0at.. The Cedric, however, is 75 feet wide, or 3 feet wider than the German ship, although her depth is only 49h feet, or more than 3 feet less than that Of her rival. The Cedric': displacement is 37,870 tons, or nearly 12,090 greater than that of the Kai Ber /1. She is well able to carry 3,000 passengers and a crew of 350, which exceeds the passenger capacity of the

THE CEDRIC, THE LARGEST ENGLISH STEAMER. German vessel. The ('drie's (!eaiweight carrying capacity will be 18,400 tons. There is no pretence of great speed put forth in her behalf, thmigh she will not be slow. She was, designed primariiy for the carriage of large cargoes of freight. Never- theless her cabin accommodations will be ample and attractive. With the Oil ric afloat, Great Britain can even be ahead of Uncle Sam in a point of greatness. for the largest ship ever constructed in America is the A-mon/and, of 12,070 tons, engaged in transatlantic trade. The new steamship Korea, belonging to the Pacific Mail Steamship Line, which not long ago started on her maiden voyage from an Francisco to Hong Kong, cost $2,000,000, and has a gross tonnage of 11,300 tons. Her speed is eighteen knots per hour, she has accommodation for 200 first class and 1,259 steerage passengers, and her crew numbers 236. The Korea carried the largest cargo ever shipped from San Francisco, 30,000 tons of freight, valued at $620.600.

WATCHING A FOOTBALL MATCH.

A Danger a Lancashire Man Found.

Out of the millions who are lovers of the football fields, of both codes, there are few who cannot learn something from the experience of ]ward Cox, aged 32 year's, an engineer's labourer at the works of Messrs. Beyer, Peacock, and Co., at Manchester. Cox was an enthusiastic follower of the Association game, and a sturdy supporter of the Manchester City Football Club through all its vicissitudes in the First Division. He is still, but a train of circumstances have made it an impossibility for him to witness a football match, although in September he followed his favourite club into the Second Division.

" I recall,and it seems a memory, the Fosse match," said Cox, chattinc, to a "Manchester Weekly Chronicle" representative, at home, 28. Gloucester-street, Gor- ton, "City won three goals to one," he added enthu- siastically. "It was when I asked him how he came to stay away after a record of attending every game," writes the reporter, "that I learned something of the dangers that lurk round the enclosure." It transpired that Cox, like a great many other goed sportsmen, are so eager to see the fray in the field that they neglect a few of the little common acts of life that stay a cold. Cox had suffered very severely from the cold, and having stood out on the groundon this bleak afternoon of September 20th he paid the penalty by being unable to leave the house again for months. The colds themselves attacked him very violently, and he was laid up with influenza, and when he recovered from this,the doctors told him that rheumatism had set in in his niuscles. The man being employed in the iron works could not think of following his employment,and was becoming low-spirited, when his mother heard of the "miraculous cure," as Mrs. Cox termed it, of Miss Baguley, aged 14, by Dr. Williams' pink pills for pale people. "Miss 13aguley's case has been the talk of the neigh- bourhood, said Mr. Cox to the reporter. "And lam glad I heard of it. I got Dr. Williams' pink pills, and the very first box got down at the awful pains in my limbs. By the time I took a second box I was just able to bend my back and stoop. That seemed like getting back to work, and I was thankful. After having a few does of the third box I went to my work, and have not been off it since. Up to now I have taken six boxes." "You look well enough now, but how are the pains?" asked the reporter. "Oh, I don't feel much of them, and I am still going to have Dr. Williams' pink pills for pale people."

THE LONDON POISONING MYSTERY.

Evidence of the Exhumation at Lymm.

The Southwark poisoning case was again investigated at Southwark on Wednesday, when Severino Klosowski, alias George Chapman, who is charged with the murder of three women, made his sixteenth appearance. Chap- man was again seated in the dock between two warders. Inspector Godley told of the exhumation of the body of Bessie Taylor (the second Mrs. Chapman), at Lymm, Cheshire, on December 22 last. William Taylor, salesman, Bessie Taylor's brother. said she was a very careful and saving woman. He first met prisoner four years ago, when his sister broughthim to Hornsey, and introduced him as her husband. Wit- ness told of the illness and death of his sister, and the burial at Lymm, which prisoner attended. Chapman said he had no money, so witness paid all the funeral expenses. A cheque for £5O, payable .to Bessie Chap- man, and which was. paid into Chapman's banking ac- count, was recognised by witness as having been drawn by his father, but witness declared that the endorsement was not in his sister's handwriting. Both witness's father and mother were well-to-do people, and Bessie was a great favourite with her father. After her death the father made another will.

In crfiss-examination witness said he knew his sister was mefftioned in his father's first will, and she must have knomp it. Frederick Richard Sanderson, County Court bailiff, said he knew Chapman and his wife very well when they were at the Grapes, Bishop Stortford,. in 1898. On one occasion, when Mrs. Chapman was ill, prisoner said he wanted his wife to make a will; and asked witness where he could get a will form. Witness told him, and Chapman fetched two forms at once. Dr. Stoker was called to prove attending Bessie Taylor. She was, according to Chapman, suffering from dyspepsia. The doctor described the patients symp- toms, which were identical with those of Miss Marsh. He had great difficulty in diagnosing the case, and had three consultations with other medical men. Dr. Stevenson, the Home Office expert, was then called, and gave evidence as to the analysis of the organs of Bessie Taylor, which he said contained a large amount of antimony. ' The hearing was then adjourned.

P irrs's ILLUSTRATED FURNISH ENG Gui DE.—Those who are thinking of furnishing would do well to write for a copy of Pitt S's Furnishing Guide for 1903. The founder and head of this renowned firm, with its exten- sive business premises in London-road and Gaythorn, i Manchester, is Mr. Samuel Pitts, who is the doyen of the furnishing trade, with a record of 57 years' expert- enoe behind him. The quality of the goods supplied by the firm may be judged from the fact that the cus- tomers include His Majesty the Sultan of Morocco, His Grace the Duke of Devonshire, K.G., mid other dis- tinguished personages. The guide is not a complete catalogue of the firms' goods but gives in a handy form the utmost information in the smallest compass of a few of the innumerable bargains which the firm, can offer in new and second-hand furniture. so that something like a fair idea may be formed of their merits. The guide is copiously illustrated, and one page of great artistic merit is a chromo-lithographed plate showing a suite which formed a portion of one of the orders Pitts's had the honour of executing for his Majesty the Sultan of Morocco. Those who are desir- ous of viewing the premises can do so and will find every convenience for doing so. The premises are heated and electrically lighted and there are hydraulic passen- ger and good lifts to all floors.

13 WEEKS IN HOSPITAL. Apparently no Hope of a Cure. • Yet Bile Beans Made Her Well.

Gallstones, Liver Disorder, and Biliousness Routed.

After suffering for many years, trying scores of pre- scriptions, passing through the hands of a succession of doctors, and being an in-patient at St. George's Hospital, London, for thirteen weeks, all without the slightest apparent benefit, Mrs, Mary Owen, of Siren House, Towyn, (Merionethshire), has been cured by Chas. Forde's Bile Beans. Here is her story

"For many years I endured agony from biliousness; indigestion, and jaundice. My liver was competely out of order, and my strength failed a last completely. , My doctor then told me that I had gallstones, and that a surgical operation was necessary if ever I wished to get better. Well, I went to St. 6eorge's Hospital, London, for the operation,but they put me underspecial treatment instead of operating, and I had to stay there for thirteen weeks. To my chagrin I got no benefit,and came from the hospital as bad went. kll this time

the pains in my chest, especially after taking any food, were terrible. The jaundice had increased until I was an abominable colour, and I was in ceaseless agony all over my right side with pain from the liver. The doctor who then attended me gave me injections of morphia,as the onlymeans of giving me relief from pain. It was while rwas. under this treatment that I passed two gall- stones. In March last a gentleman whom I had known for some years,told me that Chas. Forde's Bile Beans had done him a lot of good., Ills eithusiasm led me to try them.

The first few doses made me feel a little better, and :I determined to persevere. The improvement thus begun continued from day to day. had not long been taking Bile Beans before :E began to have a good appe- tite. I grew stronger and found the pains of indiges- tion less frequent, until they ceased altogether. The liver complaint and jaundice also disappeared from my system and the nasty bilious feelings left -me. In the end,Bile Beans cured me completely, and T luxe since enjoyed the best of health." All sufferers from liver troubles, anmmia,rheumatism, debility, headache, constipation, piles, and female ail- ments who try Bile Beans will find in them a cure. You'll get them from any chemist at Is. lad. or 2s. 9d. per box, or post free by sending prices to the Bile Bean Manufacturing Co., Red Cross-street, London, E.C.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS. Rugby Football. By " Ovalist."

A win at last ! Warrington actually defeated Batley on Saturday and gained the first try they have scored since the dawn of 1903. It was just one solitary try that did the trick, but this is no reason why a Manchester scribe should say " War- rington were lucky to win." They would have beeli unlucky to have lost, seeing that the team was weakened by the absence of Isherwood and Dickenson and that the fifteen as turned out played a gallant and determined game. There is nothing stimulates the energies Of the Warringtonians more than Batley's white jersey, q.nd,.. one can regret now that the stimu- lant was not provided earlier. The team might have had a better record.

There was no end of .(*rumbling and growling at "Warrington's luck" on Saturday morning when the news leaked out that Dickenson was unable to play through a bad throat. The prospects of the club at once went down to zero, and it is no secret that the majority of the 5,000 enthusiasts who thronged the enclosure,fully expected that the "gallant youths" would avenge the defeat sustained on October 4th, especially as they brought their full strength and a record of consistent success. But it does not do to prophesy—" oaless ye know "—as the Yankee said— and those people who backed the visitors on Saturday will have been lamenting their fate ever since. For once in a way the Warrington lads -rose to the occa- sion and defeated their doughty opponents fairly and squarely, though the margin was only a try to nil.

Winning the toss on Saturday was an advantage not to be wasted, and the homesters at once bore down on the Batley citadel with irresistible might. Burgess dashed away finely and when cornered, he somehow managed to give the forwards possession and the whole body of them swept over the line with the ball at their toes. When the top layers had been re- moved,it was found that Roberts covered the ball and he therefore was hailed as a scorer. Though Fish missed an easy goal-shot, the early success delighted the crowd and they cheered themselves hoarse. it must be remembered that those who have not been away with the team have only seen Warrington score five tries during the whole of the season, the last one at Wilderspool being on November Bth against Old- ham. No wonder there was ecstasy in the caMp when the Batley line was successfully crossed.

From now to the close of the game the play was of a fairly even nature. Both sides attacked hotly in turn,but the defence was stronger than the attack. Twice the Warrington forwards almost repeated the perform- ance which won the match, and once Wilfrid Auty had a clear run in--apparently—but the irrepressible Edmunds bobbed up from nobldy knows where, and laid the Batley Alderman's younger son by the heels. It was guineas to a gooseberry on him scoring, and the wonderful tackle of the midget, was cheered to the echo. Wattle Davies was shadowed to such an extent by Harris—who justi- fied his selection—that he scarcely got a sniff .at the ball, much less a chance of scoring. You could almost hear the fiery little Welshman grinding his teeth with rage as he was baffled and banged on the turf tune after time. Fish was receiving similar attention on the other side, but he did contrive to make headway at times,and unfortunately paid the penalty by being injured five minutes from the close.

The injury to Fish is serious, as he sustained a fracture of one'sf the small bones in his left hand, and will be out of harness for three weeks at least. With the cup ties and some stiff League matches so near,this is particularly hard luck for the 'club, which has to find a substitute for one of the regular three- quarters nearly every week.

There was a marked improvement in Warrington's play all round, and particularly forward and half back. The men in the scrummage worked like demons, and all Batley's boasted superiority here vanished like a dream. In fact Warrington were the better lot. They lasted better and were quicker in the open,than their heavier opponents. At half, Burgess and Duckworth played a fine game, and combined with an effective- ness which was most gratifying. Both were very quickly away from the pack, but Burgess occasionally marred his work by a wild pass at a critical moment. The whole Of the three-quarters did well, Edmunds and Hockenhall being quite as good as the opposing centres. Hallam was better than Higgins at full back. Oakland played a good game for Batley, and was well supported by Hodgson. Auty, -but for his failure to Score, was excellent on the wing.

Quite a number of the favourites went under on Saturday, the Champions succumbing to Hull Kings- ton Rovers, Oldham to Hurtslet, Halifax to Bradford, and Runcorn to Brighouse. The" Linnets "appear to have played as badly away,as they did well against Warrington,and were fairly beaten by their humble opponents. •

THE TRANSFER OF THE GREEN DRAGON LICENCE. Action by the Warrington and District Free Church Council.

At the annual meeting of the Warrington and Dis- trict Free Church Council, the following resolutions were passed unanimously:—" I.—That this meeting of the Warrington Free Church Council strongly condemns the action orthe magistrates in granting the transfer of the Green Dragon licence to Lovely-lane in direct opposition to the strongly expressed opinions and wishes of the inhabitants of the neighbourhood, and notwithstandhig the repeated declatation in court of the Chief Constable that in his judgment no such licence was at all necessary. The ,Council is of opinion that the magistrates, having no additional evidence to that tendered on two previous applications within the last three months, had no ground on which now to grant this transfer other than the proposal of Messrs. Greenall, Whitley, and Co., to forego two other licences in the borough, a proposal which public opinion deems hardly good enough to warrant the granting of this new licence in the teeth of suchabsolutely pro- nounced opposition on the part of those most immedi- ately concerned. ,The Council furthsr records its pro- , test against the action of the Corporation in this matter, and the appearance in court of the Deputy Town Clerk in support of the application. 2.—That in view of the general action now being taken in various parts of the country on the part of magistrates to reduce the number of existing licences. this Council expresses its earnest desire that 1.11:e licensing magistrates- of this county borough sheuld consider how far such reduction is practicable in War- rington, and refrain from any measure that would-In- crease the present facilities for the sale of intoxicatimr drink.

4.—That copies of the foregoing resolutions he sent to the churches represented on this Comicil, with a re- quest that they be read to the cogregations." _ _ _

The communication was read in all Free Churches of the town on Sunday. It was also submitted at the meeting of the Wycliffe P.S.A. Society and at the meet- ing of the Men's Class of the Workingmen's Mission Sunday School. The congregations in most cases, signified their consent, by standing.

SWINTON v. ST. HELENS.

. The return match between these clubs -•-cas played on the Swinton ground last Saturday, and resulted in an easy win for the home team. During the aftemoon,St. Helens had their lines crossed five times, and this hardly represents the run of the game, as several chanches were missed by the Swinton three-quarters right on the visitors' line. Forward, the teams ware well matched, but the visitors backs showed poor combination, and scarcely ever made a concerted. movement throughout the game. On one occasion, however, they had a splendid opportunity to reduce Swinton's lead,but Hall, with the line at his mercy, hesitated just a moment too long, and the chance was lost. There was no compari- son between the two sets of backs, and even without Valentine, who had to be carried off the field through an injury, Swinton continued to hold the upper hand. Two tries by Lewis and Valentine were scored in the first half, and in the second half Critch and Morgan made such fine openings that three more tries by Barnett. Evans, aud Oldfield wera obtained. No goals were kicked, and the game ended :—Swinton, five tries (15 points) ; St. Helens,nothing. HULL KINGSTON ROVERS v. BROUGHTON RANGERS.

This return match was played at Hull, before a good number of spectators. The first encounter early in the season ended in an easy win for the Rangers, but since that time the Rovers have played so strongly on their own ground that the Rangers with a weakened back team were not at all confident of winning. In the first half, however, they had most of the play, and Kirkham and S. James were almost over the line, but Sinclair de- fended finely, and at half-time there was no score. In the second half the Rovers held the upper hand,and their heavy forwards overplayed their opponents, with the result that scrummaging chiefly took place in the visi- tors' territory. From one of them Barry got posses- sion of the ball, dodged over the line, and scored a try for the home team, but Kemp failed at goal from a very easy place. Sinclair dropped a goal, and the Rangers were defeated by one goal and one try (five points) to nothing.

HUDDERSFIELD v. RUNCORN. Played at Huddersfield on Wednesday, and ended in a victory for Runcorn by five points to nothing.

HUNSLET v. LEIGH.

Played at Hunslet on Wednesday. and resulted in a draw of five points each.

LANCASHIRE COMBINATION.

WARRINGTON " A " V. WERNETIL. The Warringtouians secured their thirteenth succes- sive victory by defeating Werneth on Saturday at Werneth. This proved to be the only tangible record of a highly interesting match, and at the close War- riugto n had thus won by two points to nil.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

Baptist Church, Golborne:street.

The pastor commenced a series of sermons on Sun- day evening last when the church was completely filled. The subject announced was "Is one religion as good as another !' and in the course of a sermon which in- voked much interest many interesting points were emphasized. The preacher dwelt with the psychological aspect of the subject and gave many illustrations of the power of belief on character and conduct. To-morrow Mr. Dunnico will continue his discourse and will reply to correspondents.

Latchford Baptist Church.

There were large congregations at this Church on Sunday when the new organ and classroom were opened. The preacher at the morning service was the Pastor, the Rev. J. F. Matthews,and the organist, Mr. Peter Harri- son. The choir rendered the anthem "We praise Thee, 0 Jerusalem."—ln the afternoon a special service was held in the schoolroom, Mr. S. Albmson, the school superintendent, Mr. G. Wells, and Mr. Matthews taking part in it. The latter delivered a dedication address in connection with the opening of the new classroom,which the increase of the school membership has made necessary. In the evening Mr. Matthews was again the preacher, and preached a sermon to a large congregation The organist was Mr. W. H. Payton, Mus.- Bac. The choir sang the anthem "Thine. 0 Lord, is the great- ness." An organ recital was given after the service by Mr. Payton. The estimated cost of the organ and, the school extension is about £3OO. The amount already received from various sources and promised is about £240, and witha further effort it is hoped the balance will be considerably lessened.

Mr. George Heaton, of Man- chester, delivered the address and spoke upon God's help, presence and guidance. He repeated hymns by C. Wesley, Dodridge and Watts bearing upon nod's faith- fulness.—Mr. R. IL Pearson contributed a violin solo ; Mr. Joseph Owen offered prayer. and Mr. J. Ball read the lesson. At the close, the President read the communication front the Free Church Council condemning the action of the magistrates in granting the transfer of the Green Dragon licence from Bridge- street to Lovely-lane, against the expressed wish of the inhabitants of the district. He invited the men to attend the meetiug of protest, which, he understood, would be held during the week.

Catholic Philanthropic Society. IThe annual dinner in connection with the above society was held at the Patten Arms Hotel The other i evening. Invitations were sent to the clergy in the : district, viz., Fathers Whittle, Gibbons,Swarbrick (St. Alban's), Fathers Wilson, Mercer, D. D. Primevesi (St. Mary's), Father's McGrath, D'Arcy (St. Mary's, . Latchford), Father Baines (St. Benedict's), and Father Ryan (Sacred Heart). An:excellent dinner, to which 42 sat down, was provided. by Miss Kiernan. A lengthy programme of toasts and songs was gone through. Father Whittle, as chaplain of the society, submitted the toast of "His Holiness the Pope," and the toasts of "The Bishops and clergy of the dioceses of Liverpool and Shrewsbury," and "Our Society" were also honoured. The society has about 100 members and is in a prosperous condition, having funds in hand amounting to £627 17s. 101 d.

BOLTON AND THE "TIMES."

• Councillor Panton,chairman of the Bolton Electricity Committee, called attention at Wednesday's meeting of the Couccil to an article which had appeared in" The Times," in which erroneous statements as to profits from the electricity department appeared. The com- mittee did not object to honest criticism, but they ob- jected to statements which were untrue. The writer said that the Bolton Electricity Department made its profits by excessive prices, and the average profit was 2•45 d. per unit, whereas their profit was only 25d. He , (the speaker) had written a letter to "The Times " ! correcting these statements; but his communication had, ' he thought, been relegated to the waste-paper basket. This was not honourable journalism.

John Fitzgerald, who was sentenced to four years' penal servitude on Wednesday burgled a bedroom at Sussex-place, Hammersmith. where Detective-Sergeant Allen was sleeping. He was caught after a chase.
