THE NORTHERN UNION.

THE LEAGUE.---FIRST DIVISION.

WARRINGTON v. BATLEY.

Warrington were at home once more last Saturday and had Bttlev as visitors. Notwithstanding the recent failures of the "Wirepullers" there was a crowd of about 5,009 to encourage them against the "gallant youths." Unfortunately for the prospects of Warrington, Dickenson cried off, and with both the regular centres absent,Warrington's chances seemed poor, especially as Batley brought a full team. The weather was excellent and the ground in good order. The players and officials were:

Warrington: Hallam, back; Fish, Edmonds, Hocken- hull, and Harris, three-quarter backs • Duckworth and Burgess half -back; F. Swift,Morrison.iorster,Warburton Boardman, Ashton, Harmer and Naylor, forwards. Batley: Higgins, back ; Davies, .Fitzgerald, Goodall I and Auty, three-quarter backs; Oakland and F. Hodg- son, half-backs; Stubley, Phillips, Manns, H. Hodgson, Spurr, Judge, Wolstenholme and Fozzard; forwards. Referee, Mr. J. H. Cromoton, Bradford. Touch, judges, Aiessrs.E. Williams, Widnes, and J. Graham, St Helens.

Warrington won the toss and elected to take advan- tage of a strong wing which blew diagonally down the field. Davies kiaked off for Batley against the wind, and the ball went in touch at the quarter flag. Pre- mature passing caused the visitors to be penalised, and Fish found touch at the opposite quarter. Batley re- pelled Warrington for a short time, but Burgess rushed away from a scrimmage, and, throwing backwards, gave the forwards possession, Roberts dribbling over and scoring at the post five minutes from the start. Fish missed the easy place kick. Warrington were attacking again after the drop out, and some stiff scrimmages took place on the Batley line. Auty was prominent with a splendid run in thelthick of the Warrington players, but Burgess at length got him down. Fitzgerald was the next player to distinguish himself, and it was a near squeak for Warrington when Hallam floorel him, as he was close to the line and Davies was in attendance on him. Batley having got a footing in the Warrington territory, they played up with great vigour, and for a few minutes Warrington were in great danger, for they were beaten at hard scrimmaging. A fine kick by Hallam found touch at centre, and Burgess got clear away. Unfortu- nately his pass was rather wild,or a score would almost certainly have taken place. The irrepressible Burgess was again to the fore, and grounded the ball over the line but was brought back. Play now slowed down,and Warrington were not making the best of their chances when they had the wind behind. A timely judged kick by Fish, which found touch close to the corner, gave Warrington a rare chance, but the Batley forwards as- serted themselves, and Warrington were sent hack to the quarter flag. Here Batley were penalised, but Fish failed at goal, and the next minute the whistle blew for half-time. Half-time :—Warrington, 3 points; Batley, nil.

Roberts restarted, and Oakland returned to centre. Burgess got away, but he was floored by Hodgson. A penalty to Batley did not gain much ground, and the centre was the scene of operations for some time. At length,Warrington were penalised for offside play, and Davies had a shot for goal, but missed badly, and a drop out by Fish found touch at centre. Duckworth got away, and passed to Burgess who again ran finely to the Batley quarter. The visitors were soon back again, and there was some keenscrummaging onthe home line. Oakland sent the ball across, and Fish got his toe to it on the line, but kicked clean into Auty's hands. The wing man had a clear run in, but, showing little spe,ed, Edmonds dashed up and grassed him finely a yard from the line,amid tremen- dous cheering from the home crowd. Duckworth and Burgess quickly carried play to safer quarters, and by means of clever touch play they got into the Batley quarter. Here another rush by the forwards all but succeeded, Higgins Saving in the nick of time. Aided by the wind Batley attacked again, and Oakland twice kicked across for Davies,only to see the latter foiled by Harris, who fairly shadowed the famous Welshman. Then Auty got hold and made a most brilliant run right through the home team, Hallam finally bringing him down with a resolute tackle. Warrington never allowed Batley to remain long in the home quarter and the visiting forwards tiring somewhat, play ruled even in the latter stages. Warringtonfianlly won a hard game by 3 points to nil. BRIGHOUSE RANGERS v. RUNCORN.

About a thousand spectators assembled at Brighouse to witness the match between the Rangers and Runcorn. Saville started for Brighouse, and the visitors soon forced a minor. The Brighouse forwards got off with a good dribble' and Jolley was tackled in possession. A penalty kick by Saville went wide, as also did one by Farmer (Runcorn). Price got off, but his pass went wrong, and the effort failed. Ultimately Runcorn gained a foothold near the Brighouse line, and Richard- son gave a pass to Butterworth, who was tackled near the line. Baker secured,and pot in a good kick, which transferred operations to the centre, and half-time arrived without a point having been scored. A re-start was made by Farmer, and Baker was almost successful in a dash he made for goal. Runcorn were penalised, but Saville made a poor attempt. Ross feigned to pass from the scrummage, find dodged over the line with a good try. Saville landed a goal. Baker, from a pass by Denham, ran over with an apparently good try, but the referee only allowed a minor. Jolley was injund and had to be carried off the field, Foster taking his place. Price neglected a good opening' but Butterworth ran over near the corner. Farmer's kick failed. It transpired that Jolley fraotured a cartilege in the leg. Just on the call of time Ross scored a try, but Saville failed to convert. Result:—Brighouse 1 goal, 2 tries (8 points), Runcorn 1 try (3 points). Teams :

Brighouse.—Saville, back? Baker, Walker, Sutcliffe, and Smith, three-quarter backs; Ross and Denham, half backs; Kilburn, Armitage, Hodgson, Banks, Ridsdale, Woffenden, Miller, and Wilson, forwards. Runcorn.—Jolley, back; Moran, Butterworth, Price, and Jones, three-quarter backs; Richardson and Foster, half backs; Walker, Farmer, Woods, Gayter, Moulton, Higgins, C. Houghton, and S. Langley, forwards. Referee. Mr. R. H. Moore, Oldham. Touch judges, Messrs. H. W. Depledge, Huddersfleld,and H. Edmond- son, Bradford.

WIDNES v. LET.GTI. This return match was played at Widnes, before 2,000 spectators. Aspey started for Widnes against the wind, and capital combination by the home backs caused Leigh to be pressed, and Lally dodged over,and Tilley, kicked a goal less than five minutes from the start. Then Leighbad a chance. Bennett broke through the Widnes team, and passed to Milburn, who lost a certain try by failing to take the pass. Widnes relieved, and took play to the Leigh end,where Milburn got off-side, and from the ensuing penalty Tilley placed a goal. Play slowed down a lot,but eventually Molyneux placed a penalty goal for Leigh. At the interval Widnes led by seven points to two. Widnes showed up well on resuming, but the Leigh defence was sound, and then Boardman dashed away grandly only to be well tackled. After some strong attacks by Leigh,Widnes relieved,and from a good passing movement Lally scored his second try, no goal resulting. Result: Widnes. two goals and two tries (ten points) ; Leigh, one goal (two points).

HUNSLET v. OLDHAM. The return match between the Hunslet au:! Oldham teams at Parkside, Hunslet, aroused considerable interest, and the varying fortunes of an exciting game were watChed by a large crowd. At half-time, Hunslet werea couple of points in front. The Opening stages of the second half were sensational. Albert Goldthorpe landed the ball into touch at the Oldham quarter-line from the kick-off, and from the succeeding scrummage he got possession and gave to Everson, who beat his men and scored a clever try. The Hunslet captain again brought off a clevQr goal, which put his side seven points ahead. Spottiswoode eventually succeeded in crossing, after passing between the Oldham half backs. Fergus- son failed at goal. Shortly afterwards ' the scorer repeated the feat, but Fergusson was again unequal to the task of adding the goal points from near touch. and an exhilarating game ended in a victory for Hunslet by 7 points to 6—two goals and a try to two tries.

WIGAN v. HULL. At, Wigan, before 10,000 spectators. The home lot pressed at the start, and the ensuing stages were also in their favour. Hull were penalised, and Mason gave his side a two points lead. Afterwatds the visitors strove but were seldom dangerous, the half-time score being:— Wigan, one goal; Hull, none. After the interval Hull took up a strong position in their opponents' 25. Neat work by the home forwards changed the scene of opera- tions, and Jacques was forced to touch down. Hull put on pressure, and Moxon received, and scored at the corner, Jaques failing at goal. Final :—Hull, one try (three points); Wigan, one goal (two points). HUDDERSFIELD v. SALFORD.

STOCKTON HEATH.

--- MEETING OF THE PAROCHIAL COMMITTEE. THE SMALLPDX CASES. Mr. Mountield presided at the meeting of the Parochial Committee held on Thursday evening. There were also present :—Messrs. Pearson, Pemberton, Fair- hurst, Whitley, Mclntyre, with Mr. Hough, (deputy clerk), Mr. Swinton (surveyor), and Mr. Farrington .(nuisance inspector).—Mr. Swinton submitted for approval plans of 14 houses and a storehouse for Mr. Chappell in Gibson-street, off Warburton-street. —The plans were approved.—The Nuisance Inspector reported that a second case of smallpox had occurred in the village and the patient had been isolated at the hospital at Moore.—lt was decided that the Inspector should visit Stockton Heath once a week to receive and investigate complaints.;—Certiticates of occupation were granted to Mr. Thomas Williams for 8 houses and to Mr. Frank Warburton for two villas in Walton-road. —ln answer to an inquiry, the Chairman stated that three cases of small-pox had taken place on the male side of the Workhouse at Dutton.

. Lady Lawson, wife of Sir Charles Lawson, having consented to go into .an inebriates' home, the charge agahtst her of annoying the dwellers at 15, Evelyn [Gardens, Kensington, was withdrawn at West Loudow on Wednesday.

THE EXAMINE K.

FEBRUARY 6th, 1903.;

"THERE are some • BUDGET PROSPEDTS. marvellous fine pro-

spects in Scotland," said BOSINELL to JOHNSON, with the result of pro— voking a famous retort. There are no marvellous fine prospects connected with Mr. RITCHIE'S first Budget, we regret to state. There are sanguine persons who expect large remissions of taxation.. They are demanding a considerable reduction in the income-tax. The were taking of 2d. or 3d. in the the Government is told by its supporters in the Press, will not only be insufficient, but will be irritating. The present fifteenpence must be reduced to 9d., or 10d. at most. Even then the income-tax will stand exceptionally high. There is not the least likelihood, however, that Mr. RiTourE will be able to take rid. or 6.d. in the from the tax which is ono of the principal and most convenient sources of revenue. If he were an enthusiast for sound finance ha would probably be at this moment arranging to leave the income-tax alone. For the purposes of the late war the Government has placed addi- tional taxation on the breakfast table. It has also taxed the poor man's bread. The new levies on corn, sugar,and tea should be abandoned before the income-tax is touched; but so little does the country expect heroic finance from a country like the present that there is actually no expectation or hope of relief id those varieties of taxation which fail with peculiar weight on the industrious poor. On the contrary,there is speculation on the probability that he will place a further ')urden on tea. The payer of income-tax has been told also by the present CHANCELLOR of the EXCHEQUER and by his predecessor that he shall have the first relief that it is possible to give, and as a new Chancellor is likely to strive hard to make a popu- lar Budget, Mr. ltirourE will probably endeavour to reduce the fifteenperice to a shilling. A singular instance of the low quality of some present-day patriotism is supplied by the fact that the 1.10,000,000 which are this year to come from South Africa are expected to be used for the purpose of lightening taxation. To use the money in that way would be utterly despicable, and a fraud on posterity. The £10,000,000 that are to be paid Intl the ExcEEQuEa, on .account of S Rah Africa are a part of the Rand contribution to the costs of the war, and a Hcrise of Commons which per- mits them to be used for any other purpose than the reduction of the war debt will expose itself to the contempt of mankind. Mr. Rrreniz is believed to be a strong and a straightforward man. These are the political virtues which account for his rise. He has no others. But the stronger and the more straightforward he is the more he 38 likely to die

===== 0002601_19030206_art0070.txt | included=true | included =====
-1

appoint expectation by his Budget.. If he faces the actual situation honestly and boldly, he will have to acknowledge to himself ana the country that remissions of taxation can only be made pos- sible by the evasion of liabilities.

If we had a CHANCELLOR of the EXCHEQUER like Mr. GLADSTONE, he would be talking, not of remitting taxation, but of clearing off the debt for the war. However, it must be admitted in regard to Mr. RITCHIE that he is in a new and unprecedented situation. No previous CHANCELLOR of the EXCHEQUER has had to -deal with such a colossal sum in the way of normal revenue. The yield for the year was estimated at £152,435,000. It is considerably less than twenty years 'since a Liberal Government was overthrown because,under exceptional circumstances, it brought in what was known as "the hundred-million Budget." Considerably less than twenty years before Mr. CHILDERS came to grief over his financial pro- posals, Mr. GLADSTONE had offered to abolish the income-tax his ability to do this being based on what then seemed the reasonable understanding that £70,000,000 annually is 'ample provision for carrying on the Government of the country. The estimated expenditure for the financial year which is shortly about to close, Without taking into account the heavy burdens Which have been left by the war,was £134,609,000. This is enormous, but it will soon cease to be ex- ceptional. Expenditure is increasing all round. All the services are constantly becoming more costly. A bounding revenue has encouraged ex- travagance, and extravagance has hardened into ' a habit. How, when revenue has ceased to ex- pand, and when we have a _prospect of lean years before us, are we to sustain the vast burden of taxation? To that question only a vague ana bewildering answer can be given. Sir MICHAEL RICKS-BEACH'S inability to furnish a reply led to his resignation. 000 CHANCELLOR Of the Ex..- CHEQUER after another will find himself flounder- ing from morass to morass until the country takes the matter into its own hands, and instructs the Government that the financial policy of the future must be the old and time-honoured policy of the Liberal party—economy, retrenchment, and reform.

In another column Mr. Joynson Hicks frankly apologises for his remark at the recent Unionist meeting in Warrington: "Thank God Ido not know Mr. Crosfield: Such a deplorable exhibi- tion of bad taste is not at all excusable, but we are quite willing to accept Mr. Hicks' explanation, though we must confess to being puzzled to know in what "political sense" Finch a r3mark could be applied. However, the incident is closed now that Mr. Hicks has acknow- ledged his fault and atoned for it to the best of his ability. Unfortunately such remarks are not readily forgotten, and we hope the recollection of What has occurred will prevent Mr. Hicks from being, in the heat of a future political harangue, again betrayed into the thoughtless use of so offen- eive an expression. The arguments he further advances in his letter, respecting the cause of the War, we can safelrleave Mr. Parker or Mr. Cros- fAld to refute if they think it necessary. "The greatest colonial minister" is believed by a great many studious and fair-minded people to have been largely responsible for the ultimate outbreak of war, and if his friends choose to look upon this attitude as "a veiled charge of wholesale murder" they are at liberty to do so. The interpretation ja their own, and Mr. Chamberlain would prob- 1111Y not thank them for bawling it from the 'use-tops.

NOTES BY THE WAY AT

EARLESTOWN.

(B-v A CORRESPONDENT j.

The proceedings at the District Council meeting on Monday were of a distinctly lively chamcter,the trouble arising in connection with the fiasco at the recent fire. Several of the members who took part in the discussion had evidently become possessed of certain facts, which did not tend to improve matters, and with such a whip they did not forget to lash out right and left. The Chairman of the Fire Brigade Committee tried to get Out of the responsibility for the muddle on the ground that he had only occupied that position two months. Bt.it he overlooked the maxim that "Where there's a Will, there's a way," and a lot of good and useful work teal' be done in two months. He had evidently never .roubled to ascertain whether everything was in work- Order, shnuld a fire occur, but with the rest of the officials calmly waited for a fire to take place, in order that by experience they might learn what was the rroper thing to do. They were somewhat like the seven i°olish virgins of biblical fame, and have suffered accordingly.

tc,1,1 the estimation of the ratepayers, the proper thing. c-nun is pass a vote of want of confidence in the Water eq`,4lltnittee and the Fire Brigade Committee.—The airman of the Fire Brigade Committee made "Amend 4 honorable," when he accepted re- ronsibility on behalf of the officials for the .„nlentable state of affairs, which had made the Council 'ne laughing stock of neighbouring districts. To have 'ln "engine which won't work, and water which won't F°lne when wanted is truly an enviable position to be hut the 'experience" gained, will perhaps liven 'flings up a bit, for a while at any rate. It is unfor- tunate the incident happened just as the elections are under consideration.

I notice frora•the proceedings of the Council that Dr 'Latham has decided to withdraw his motion about the Common resolution. He has been well advised.

FOOTBALL.

Wonders never cease! The Earlestown team Went to St. Helens on Saturday, saw and conquered. The Rees. were their adversaries, and everybody, even the man in the street. prognosticated a sound thrashing. Events Proved, however, that the man in the street was wrong, for the thrashing was on the. other back. Again were the inconsistencies of football realised. The team was again changed, and another goalkeeper, named Parting- ton appeared between the sticks, and right well he Shaped, in fact, from what is stated, the. space between the posts might have been filled in, for all the chance the Rees had of getting the hall into the net. Martin, centre half, was another new one, and he fully satisfied the critics. Cox was again tried, and has evidently now earned a permanent place in the team, whilst Stanton, who has had a rest, played a sterling game, and quite pleased his supporters.

To beat the Rees on their own ground by 2 goals to is-no mean feat, but by superior play, this was what i%arlestown achieved, and nobody was more surprised tbilan the Rees • themselves, who after the experience 'Arlestown had at Darwen the previous week, counted Pon gaining a couple of points, and disappointment was "iecordingly keen. Earlestown set a very fast pace at lee kick off, and maintained it to the end, the play -pm the commencement being that of a. winning team. .tt is to be hoped their tide of luck has turned and that the committee will have wisdom enough to keep the team together. Nothing so demoralises a team as the onstant changing of its members, and now that they liave got 11 good men, they should keep them.

To-morrow, Saturday, they have Darwen as visitors, bat from circumstances beyond the control of the 'committee, it is hardly likely that the team which keat the Rees will be able to take the field in its en- 'ray. It is probable that Limond and Martin will be bsentees, which is most unfortunate.

RUNCORN ANUNIDNES.

A WINDING UP ORDKR.

A compulsory order was made in the Chancery Division of the High Court of Justice on Tuesday for the winding up of W. R. Earp and Co., Limited, who curried on business at Runcorn. The petitioner was C. A. Slater, an unsatisfied judgment creditor for £950.

RUNCORN BOATING CLUB DANCE. The Runcogn Boating,Club held the last dance of the season in the Brunner Guild Hall on Friday last. The room had, been tastefully decorated, dancing commenc- ing at 9 o'clock to the music a Mr. Leahs string band. Mr. R. P. Johnson officiated as M.C., Mr. James Lea of the Temperance Hotel, being responsible for the refresh- ments.

RUNCORN CO--OPERATIVE ELECTION. On Saturday an election took place for three members in place of three retiring members of the Co-operative Stores Committee. The three retiring members were re-elected, the actual figures being :—Watson, 385; Sera= 335' Rawlinson, 371; Stubbs 316; Lloyd, 308 ; Clarke, 108. The first three were elected.

A RUNCORN LADEN VESSEL ASHORE. Early on Monday morning, during the gale, the three- masted schooner Lancashire Lad, of Fleetwood, with salt from Runcorn to Morrison Haven, was driven ashore in Thurso Bay. Captain Roberts and the crew took to the rigging,from which Edward Williams,a boy, and a native of New, ,Brighton, was washed overboard and lost. After long exposure the crew were rescued by the rocket apparatus, the mate suffering a broken arm.

RUNCORN GUILD IMPROVEMENT CLASS. Under the presidency of Mr. IL H. Posnett, Mr. D. Moore delivered a paper on "The decline of the British Empire." In a very lucid, closely reassured manner the essayist showed that public and secret vices were fast ruining the life of the nation and that national life must dec,ay,if commerce,art,and society were not speedily impregnated with a new morality. In the subsequent discussion Messrs. F... Rowlands, W. Millington, J. W. Leathwood, the chairman and others took part.

HUMOROUS SINGING COM. 'EST..

This event-. took place the Public Hall, Runcorn, on Saturday evening and was a great success in every way. The majority of the performers were men of high ,rank in the humorous singing world. The result was as follows :-Ist and 2nd prizes equally divided between Alf. B. Millard (of Manchester) and Jack Ward (of Bolton) ; 3rd, Mark Tyme (of Rusholme, Manchester) ; 4th, Fred Horsfall (of Leeds) ; sth, divided between Harrison Hall (of Manchester) and Clifford Lloyd (of Leeds). Mr. E. Beleam (of Hyde) Was the adjudica- tor.

AtLEG ED THEFT._

At the Runcorn Police Court on Friday, before Mr. James Pritchard, Joseph Spencer, an elderly man, with about twenty aliases, was charged on remand with fraudulently obtaining board and lodgings from Martha Robinson, of 25, Parker-street.—The evidence showed that the prisoner went to the house of Mrs. Robinson, a widow, and made a statement on the strength of which she provided him with food and lodging to the value of 14s. He then decamped.—Prisoner was then charged with stealing a watch and chain belonging to Christopher Melling.—Froni the evidence it appeared that Melling was a lodger at Mrs. Robinson's and that the prisoner had taken the watch when he left the house.—Prisoner who has served two terms of five years, was committed to the Quarter Sessions.

===== 0002601_19030206_art0076.txt | included=true | included =====
A RUNCORN MYSTERY.

On Wednesday, at Sutton, near Runcorn; Mr. Dobson held an inquest on the body of anewly-born child found on Tuesday in the Weaver Canal by Harry Critchley, labourer, Runcorn.—Dr. Carruthers, Runcorn, said that in conjunction with Dr. Selby, Frodsham, he made a post-mortem examination of the body. It was that of a recently-born, fully-developed female child. It had not received any attentiOn at birth. He was of opinion that the child was born alive. It was impossible to say whether the child was alive or dead when it got into the water.—John Pemberton, Sutton, said that between eight and nine on the morning of the 12th January he saw-Margaret Gough, or Spencer, sitting on a stone near his stables. Later' from what was said to him, he went to the place where he had previously seen the woman and there saw a quantity of blood on the ground.—Dr. Selby said that on January 12th Margaret Gough was admitted to Dutton Workhouse Hospital.—The Coroner adjourned the inquiry in order that Margaret Gough should be present. PRESENTATION TO THE W.V. N. J. POOLE. The Rev. N. J. Poole, who this week leaves the curacy of Holy Trinity Church, Runcorn, to take up a similar position at St. Peter's Church Birken- head, was on Sikturday, the recipient of a handsome secretaire, fro he members of the Holy Trinity Men's Bible Class. The Vicar (the Rev. C. R. Killia) pre- sided over a good attendance, which included Messrs. Wilding, W. J. Cunningham, W. H. Grice, W. Banks, and J. Bailey (secretary). After eulogistic speeches from Messrs. Wilding, Once, Banks, and Killcross, Mr. Bailey made the presentation to the rev, gentleman in felicitous terms.—The Rev. N. J. Poole in appropriately responding,thanked them for their kindness and referred to the rich experience of the past four years during which he had laboured amongst them.—After the Vicar, the Rev. C. R. Killick, and Mr. Cunningham had ad- dressed the meeting, refreshments were handed round by Mesdames Cunningham, and J. Bailey, and the Misses J. Horsefleld, Johnson, A. and F. Annett and . E. and A, Turner. On Sunday evening Mr. Poole preached his farewell sermon.

A letter was read from the Runcorn Rural District Council stating that they were not satisfied with the quality of the water supplied in the parishes of Halton and Weston, and asking what price the Urban District Council require for the water mains, Ste., in the said parishes, so that they could provide their own supply. . .

YOUNC: THIEVES AT RUNCORN.

At the Runcorn Police Court on Friday,before Messrs. F. J. Norman and James Pritchard, Frederick Johnson (12) and Albert Butterworth (8) were charged with stealing a pair of clogs, valued at 2s. from the shop of Thomas Crosby, in Devonshire-square, on the 29th ult. The evidence showed that the lads were in Devonshire- square, went to the shop of Mr. Imison and offered the clogs in pledge. Suspecting that they had come by the clogs dishonestly he called in P.C. Oakes who ascer- tained that the clogs had been stolen from Mr. Crosby's shop.—The lads were ordered to receive six strokes of the birch rod each.

RUNCORN NOTES.

[BY A COItRESPONDEF.I

The Rural Council are by no means satisfied with the quality of the water supplied to Halton and Weston. They have therefore written to the Urban Council ask lug the price at which they will sell the water mains &c. The letter has been referred to the Water Com- mittee and meanwhile the net result of the negotiations is being eagerly awaited in view of the contradictoxt reports by experts as to the quality (If the water.

The small-pox,judging by the Medical Officer's report, - seems to have been satisfactorily stamped out. One remarkable statement which Dr. MacDougall brings to . .

light in his report is that the man who Went fromi the lodging-house to the Union could not by any means have caught the disease from the lodging-house and con- veyed it to the Union. If this be correct, then every- one must have been wrong in blaming the Urban Authority for allowing infected persons to enter the Union. Happily no fresh Runcorn cases have been repor- ted, and it is a matter for thankfulneus that the plague has been eradicated.

Everyone will be pleased to learn that Mr. Booth, secretary of the Runcorn Cycle Club, was the recipient of a substantial memento of his services in connection with the Club and the Cottage Hospital Fund. No one, save two or three, knows the vast amount of Work that Mr. Booth has done in connection with that worthy object he and his club have so persistently kept in view. How that the culmination of their efforts is about to be realized it is fitting that he should be the man the club delight to honour, though, there is no doubt, that the permanent nature of the work accomplished will be Mr. Booth's best reward:

Speaking at a local Church meeting the other day, Canon Wood in praising the inquitous Education Bil 4 actually bad the temerity to assert that even a bitter opponent like Mr. Lloyd George now agreed with the bill and thought it. a step in the right direction. How Canon Wobd can attempt to convey such an erroneous impression passes the wit of man. In a Liberal demon- stration in Dundee held almost simultaneously to the Vicar's meeting, Mr. Lloyd-George, M.P., said dealing with the English Education Act, 'it was simply a great attempt on the part of the Episcopacy, supported by the Government, to get dominion over the consciences of the children. It was a confederacy between parsi- mony and priestcraft for the purpose of starving and enslaving the souls of the- children of the people. If they wanted religious education let the Bible be given and the priest cleared out." In view of these strong statements it does not look as though Mr. Lloyd-George regarded the Act withinuch favour. Perhaps the Vicar will kindly explain in his usually sauve manner the apparent descrepancy.

A meeting of the Cottage Hospital Committee Was held the other day, when it was decided to name the building "The Victoria Memorial Infirmary." It is anticipated that the building will be opened some time next month.

The latest official report shows that the building of the transporter proceeds apace. There has been some small talk in Runcorn and Widnes that •the work will not be completed to time, but there is every reason to believe that such croakers will be completely silenced ere long.

RUNCORN URBAN DISTRICT COUNCIL.

The monthly meeting of the above Council was held on Wednesday in the Council Chamber. Present :—Mr. S. Taylor (in the chair), 10. Wfswall, Kirkham, T. Dean, .1. Wilson, T. Jones, J. Salkeld, M. Sweetlove, D. Bis- brown, and W. Handley with Messrs. F. A. Lake (law clerk), E.lMarshall, (secretary); J. Wilding, (surveyor); and Dr. McDougall (medical officer).

THE SMALL-PDX SCARE IN RUNCORN.

The Secretary read the following special report pre- sented by the medical odicer :—" Since the last meeting of the Council two cases of small-pox have been under treatment at the Isolation Hospital. The first'cose is that ofla man aged 49 years and occurred in a coramon lodging house in Fryer-street. He appears to have been some- what unwell on the Bth of January. On the morning of the 9th he was promptly isolated and taken to the Infectious Hospital forthwith. where he developed small-pox of a moderately mild type and has since convalesedpatisfaCterily. This man was no doubt in contact with the tramp who left the same lodging house on the morninr, of 23rd December. On the morning of January 17th bI received an intimation that a woman who resided in the north east part of the town up to the eighth of January, but from the after- noon of that date until the 11th was in close attendance on a case of small-pox in another part of the country was removed on the evening 'of the 11th in the • same van in which the small-pox case was removed to the Isolation Hospital, quarantined in the caretaker's house until the following Friday the and was then allowed to return home. She was :vaccinated and other means of protection had been adopted before she was allowed her liberty. Since I got to know of the case, inquiries were set afoot, at once, and from my observa- tion of the case I was satisfied that it was one that ought to be isolated forthwith. After some parley she consented to go to the Isolation Hospital. The eruption of small-pox soon developed and the case reached its climax in the usual way and she is now convalescing satisfactorily. The common lodging house from which the first case was received was immediately :Ater the removal of the patient thoroughly disinfec- ted from top ceiling to bottom floor, and every possible precaution was 'taken to prevent the spread of such a loathsome disease. When the roll call was run over it was found that about 28 Of the inmates made the lodg- ing-houSe their home and had made it their home for some time. • To turn such a large number of them adrift was too serious a matter to be thought of, for at that time some of the inmates might be in an incubatory stage of the disease either from having been in contact with the quarryman or with the one more recently removed to the' Isolation Hospital. A correct register was: prepared of all the inmates that had made the lodging-house .their home, and the landlady who had , rendered invaluable assistance throughout willingly I agreed to exclude all new comers for three weeks and it is only within the last day or two that fresh lodgers have been admitted. The authority have no plenary powers in dealing with lodging houses and they or their representatives have no power to interfere so long as the licensee conforms to the terms .of the licence and the requirements of the common lodging house bye-laws. All that was possible under the cir- cumstances was done to prevent a spread of the disease and with eminently satisfactory results. .The house from which the, other case was removed was thorougly disinfected and there has been no fresh development from that quarter, now eighteen days since the case was removed to hospital. The authority reasonably hopes that the town is once more free from the ! ravages of so terrible a scourge and from any further risk to the well-being of the inhabitants at all events so far as the cases under treatment are concerned and those with whom they were in contact before re- moval. The lodging houses of the town have been and are under constant surveillence and will remain for some time. On the morning of the 23rd December about ten o'clock the unfortunate man who has been the alleged cause of so much ferment, left the lodging house telling the landlady that he was going to Chester, and it appears that he took what he thought to be the nearest route by way of St. Helens. In the afternoon of the same day as the person who has since sickened of small-pox turned up at the lodging house. in a. sickening condition of intoxication, and out of pity, and as an act of grace the good-natured landlady allowed him to rest on the floor of the kitchen for the night. As a reward for her kindness he left behind him several acts of forget- fulness which must have causedhim. some anxiety in the early morning for he left before the landlady got into the kitchen although she was there according to her own statement shortly after six o'clock. He has not been back since to make amends. He was not at the lodging-house for some time before the night in ques- tion,and has not been sitice, and it appears that he never was and could not be in contact with the infected man already referred to. It is well-known that he was else- where during a part of his holiday and might have con- tracted the infection there. His movements are not always well-known and his veracity I fear is some- times at all events open to doubt. The Chairman said that the report was a very satis- factory one and great Credit was due to Dr.Machougal. It was perfectly clear that there were no other cases. The Medical Officer said there had been no fresh developments and it would be a most unusual thing if there was any outbreak eighteen days after the last contact. _ _ •

..... Mr. Dean remarked that it had gone the rounds of the papers that the man taken to Ur Workhouse had carried the infection there from a lodging-house. The Medical Officer's report showed that the man had not caught the ,infection there at all, so the Rural Authority could not say that the town had sent .a small-poK case to the Workhouse. DEATH OF DR. J. W. WATKINS, EARLESTOWN. The First Chloroform Patient.

Many of our readers will regret to hear of the death of Dr. J. W. Watkins, of Kirkby, Newton-le-Willows. He was seized with a stroke early onrriday morning last, followed by a second seizifre about 2 o'clock in the afternoon, the effects of the latter proving fatal.

_ - The deceased was born at Towcester, Northampton- shire on the 15th May, 1833, being the son of Thomas Watkins, surgeon of that town. He was educated at Brackley Grammar School, and commenced his medical career as a pupil of Dr. Percival, of Northampton. At the same time he attended the Infirmary. He further studied at the University College Hospital, London and Edinburgh University, and took his degree in 1856, lhaving previously qualified M.R,C.S. At Edinburgh Ihe volunteered to be placed under'chloroform at the request of and under the care of Sir James Simpson, the ' drug at this time having only just been introduced. and he was the first person to be subjected to its effects. He afterwards wrote his " Thesis " for his M.D. on "Its Administration in Sur- gical Cases." He practised in the neighbourhood of Towcester and for his services during an outbreak of Asiatic cholera was Presented with a gold medal by the cottagers of Grafton Regis. He afterwards went to Healey as assistant to Dr. Murrel. He came to Newton in 1857 and practised there np to the time of his death. In the '6o's he was appointed medical officer and public vaccinator under the Warrington Union but retired on a pension after 32 years' service. He was also Medical Officer of Health to the Newton-in-Makerfield Urban District Council from 1873 up to his death and for many years occupied a siniilar position under the Hay- dock authority. He was also certifying factory surgeon for the district,and medical officer to the Liverpool Farm School. ,From 1887 to 1888 he was president of the Lancashire and Cheshire branch of the British Medical Association, and was a member of the Council of this association at the time of his death. He took an active part in relieving the sufferers by the Woodpit explosion and was appointed medical officer to the fund. For 20 years .he was a member of the Ist South Lancashire Regiment of Volun- teers, subsequently as Surgeon Major, and on his retirement received the Volunteer' decora- tion. He acted as correspondent to St. Peters schools Newton-le-Willows for 40 years. He was clerk and treasurer to the Dean School Exhibition Charity since its resuscitation, hon. secretary of Warrington Training College until he was compelled to give up this office as the result of a serious accident, but he remained a member of the committee. He was a member of the committee of Clergy Daughters school, Warrington, a member of the Liverpool Diocesan Finance Association for the Winwick Rural deanery. He was selected by the Bishop of Liverpool from the I,iverpool Diocesan Association to the House of Laymen of York. He was on the committee when the chancel was erected at St. Peter's ChurchiNewton. He was married in 1859 to Harriet Bromehead, eldest daughter of Richard Heyes of Mornington Place, London. His wife died in 1896. He never quite recovered from the effects of his accident in 1887 and since his wife's death he gradually failed in health. His sudden seizure of the 30th January was quite unexpected._ _

===== 0002601_19030206_art0083.txt | included=true | included =====
THg FUNERAL.

The funeral took place on Tuesday- morning, the ser- vice being conducted at St. Peter's Church by the Rev. J. Ryder assisted by the Rev. W. Rowlands, M.A.' a former curate of the church. The coffin was placed in the church in early morning. There was a large attend- ance of the general public both inside the church and at the cemetery. Mr. E. Leyland played "0 rest in the Lord "whilst the choir sang Psalm 90 "The Lord has been our refuge ;.the hymn being "On the resurrection morning." The ordinary burial service of the church of England was read. At the conclusion of the Service the organist played the Dead March in "Saul," The coffin was then borne down the centre aisle by members of the Good Intent Lodge of Oddfellows of which the deceased was both medical_ attendant and also a financial member. The procession to the cemetery was headed by the following members of the District Council :—C. B. F. BorrOn, NV. Coll i ngwood, T. Ormand, J. W. Emmett, Dr. Patterson, Dr. Latham, N. Siddeley, W. Gilmour, A. Smith, E. L. Bent, J. Simpson, F. J. Wood and E. T. Evans • C. Cole (clerk), R. T. Surtees (water engineer) and H. Houghton- (collector.) The chief mourners were :—Dr. Harold and Mrs. Watkins, Mr. and Mrs. Cecil Watkins, Rev. Allan Watkins of St. James's Church Regent-street, Huddersfield Dr. Arthur Watkins of Huddersfield, Dr. Bernard Watkins of NVhitchurch, Miss Edith Watkins. and Dr. and Mrs. Overton. Representing the clergy were Revs: T. R. Matthews (St. John's) A. M. Mitchell (Burton- wood), R. Smith (Lowton), J, Allatt (Congregational), C. E. Dove (Wesleyan) and H. Siddall (Ashton). The medical profession was represented by Drs. Hay- ward (Haydock); Handyside, Alouncey, Street (Haydock Lodge), Dobb (Golborne), Worsley (Golborne). Almost the whole of the prominent residents of Newton-le- Willows entered the procession amongst the most not- able being, N.-Stone J.P., Colonel J. L. Wood J.P., R. Barton, T. J. Gillespie J.P., A. C. Leslie, P. Fairclough, G. Airev, NV. Bloomfield Bare Hindley &filth, Snidney Smith, Douglas Smith, and C. L. Penrhyn representing Canon Penrhyn. The coffin was of plain oak with brass mountings. A brass plate on the coffin bore the inscription, 'John Webb Watkins, born May 15th, 1833, died January 30th. -1903." Between 60 and 70 wreaths were sent, want of space preventing a list of the senders being given. DR. WATKINS' PUBLIC SERVICES. SPEECHES AT THE URBAN DISTRICT COUNCIL

MEETING.

At the monthly Meeting of the Newton Urban Dis- trict, Connell on Monday evening, • The Chairman (Mr. W. Collingwocid) said that be- fore commencing the business,he felt it was his duty to refer to the very sad and sudden loss which not only the Cowicil,but the township in general had sugtaiaed by the death of Dr. Watkins. They as a Council, he was sure felt that by the death of their medical officer they had lost a man who had served them in such a way that they could not have desired any better. Words failed him to express what, his feelings were,because he felt the loss.very much personilly. and it had come so. guddenly. The agenda for. that meeting contained the last :report which the late Dr. Watkins presented only a few days ago-. He would ask the Council to pass a vote of condolence with the family and relatives of the de- ceased gentleman. Mr. J. W. Emmettsaid he had known the late Dr. Watkins for about thirty five and a half years. During that time the deceased gentlernanhad always tried to do his duty to the townships and so far as he (the speaker) - knew, he succeeded very well indeed. Dr. Watkins was also the medical officer for the Londonand North-Western Railway Waggon Department and there- fore he (Mr. Emmett) came into contact with him a great deal.. He the speaker) could say nothing but words of praise in regard to the manner in which Dr. . Watkins discharged his duties in that department. Mr. Ormand said he had been a member of the old Board of Commissioners and of that Council for thirteen years and during that time had frequently had to con- sider reports by Dr. Watkins( Those reports had been marvels of information and had been drawn up in such . a manner, as from time to time to evoke the admire- tion.Of everyone who had read thorn. Dr. Watkins had had some difficult and disagreeable duties to discharge at times, but he had invariably discharged them with tact and courtesy,and although disagreeable, the work had been done fearlessly,faithfully,and well. Along with Mr. Emmett, Mr. Borron and himself, Dr. Watkins was the Council's representative on the Winwick Edu cational Foundation, and in connection with that trust he had very faithfully discharged his duties which during the past; few years had been very onerous indeed. The zeal, skill, and ability exhibited by Dr. Watkins, and the fund of information at his disposal had been of very great assistance to the governors of that foundation. As the hon. secretary he (Mr. Ormand) wished to express his personal indebt- edness for the information he had received from Dr' Watkins. He thought they would be lacking in the dis- charge of a duty which they owed to Dr. Warkins, if 1 they did not place upon record for the remembrance•of his family the very splendidwork he had done on behalf of education in the township. I The motion was carried in silence.

Questions and Explanations. Who is to Blame ?

Mr. W. Collingwood presided at the monthly meeting of the Newton Urban District Council which was held at the Town Hall, Earlestown, on Monday evening. There were also present :—Messrs. C. B. F. Borron, J. W. Emmett, W. Gilmour, E. L. Bent, E. T. Evans, T. Ormand, N. Siddeley, J. Simpson, A. Smith, F. J. Wood, Dr. Patterson, Dr. Latham with C. Cole (clerk), R. T. Surtees (gas and water engineer) and T. Bowes (surveyor).

===== 0002601_19030206_art0086.txt | included=true | included =====
THE LATE MRS. JAMES SMITH.

Mr. A. Smith said that on behalf of his brothers and sisters and himself he thanked the Council very heart- ily for the very kind vote they passed in reference to his mother at the last meeting. MR. THOMPSON'S ABSENCE.

The Chairman informed the Council that the reason why Mr. Thompson StilS not present was that he was staying in Scotland for the benefit of his health. . THE QUALITY OF THE GAS. .At a meeting of the Gas Committee, the Engineer reported with respect. to the illuminating power of the gas supplied to consumers in past years, and that the illuminating power prescribed by the Council in 1895 was 20 candles. It was resolved that the matter be left for the consiileration' of the Chairman of the Committee, and that in the meantime the Engineer be directed to take steps for improving the quality of the 'gas. The tenders which had been received for carting coal from Messrs. Evans and Co.'s siding and the Earlestown goods station to the gas works,for the period eliding 31st March, 1905, were submitted to the Committee, when it was -resolved that the tender of Mr. W. Long- ton be accepted. The minutes were confirmed. THE WATER UNDERTAKING. THE RECENT DISASTROUS FIRE AT EARLESTOWN. INTERESTING DISCUSSION.

At a meeting of the Water Committee it was re- solved that the engineer be directed to obtain and fix,as soon as possible, a sufficient number of wash-out and air-valves, so that the mains may be • periodically cleansed. A letter was read at a meeting of the Water Special Sub-committee from the Local Government, Board,enclosing their sanction (dated the 23rd Decem- ber, 1902) to the borrowing of the sum of £18,679 for the purposes of the Council's water undertaking, such loan to be borrowed on the security of the revenue, fund and rate which the council are authorised to mortgage for the purpose of securing the repayment of the said sum and to be repaid together with the interest thereon within a period not exceeding 21 years from the date of the borrowing thereof. The Local Government Board also advised that the proposals of the Council should be amended in certain particulars, and for that purpose they returned the plans for amendment. The Clerk reported that the plans had been sent to the engineers, together with a copy of the Local Government Board's recommendations and read a letter from the engineers upon the abject. The engineers reported to the sub-committee with res- pect to the tenders for the supply and erection of the new engines or the pumping station and submitted analysis of such tenders. They also stated that the tenders would be carefully considered by them before finally reporting thereon to the committee. The engineers were directed. to report to the committee with respect to the recommendations of the Local Government Board as to the• steining of the well, and other matters referred to by the board. As regards the question of the water from the new well passing, through the surface condenser, the engineers stated that they would further constder the matter. The engineer reported that Dr. J. Campbell Brown, County Analyst, had made an analysis of a sample of the water from the grey sandstone rock, taken from the heading at a depth of 190 feet, from which it appeared that its hardness was 180. It was resolved that the 'question of the obtaining of a supply of water from the London and North-Western Railway Coinpany, to enable a 24 hours' supply to be given to consumers, and also as to the driving of further headings, and the desirability of acquiring additional land for that purpose, be referred to the Chairman of this committee and Councillors W. Latham and T. Orman.d. In moving the adoption of the minutes Mr. Borron made some observations upon "the calamity which had happened in the district last week." In reference to the short supply pf water, the Council would be aware that for some considerable time they had been unable to distribute through the neighbourhood a full and ample supply for twenty-four hours each day, limiting that supply to domestic uses only. Before the fire took place,the sub-committee adopted a resolution that they would take steps at once to give instructions that a ' water supply in the district for twenty-four hours each day, should be arranged for. They applied to the London and North-Western Railway Company for a certain supply, so as to enable them to carry out the arrangement and that was granted. The engineer having had many complaints of the disagreeableness of the water owing to sand and other deposits in the pipes,was making various experiments to remove the deposit before the additional supply was provided. These experiments had unfortunately not concluded before the fire overtook them. He was pleased to say that a full and ample supply for twenty-four hours per day was now being obtained from thelwells and boreholes,and through the intervention of Mr. Emmett,the Council had authority to take a supply from the London and North-Western Railway Company to such extent that they could afford, when the Council supply failed. He trusted that the Council would never again find itself unprepared to deal with the full requirements of the district. Mr. Gilmour observed that with all deference to what Mr. Bonen had said, it looked strange in the eyes of the public that there should be an abundance of water after the fire had occurred.

A Member: It is soft water, Mr. Gilmour said that whether it was soft water or not there ought to have been sufficient water to cope with the fire. They were looking more after whiskey than water. - Mr. Emmett said the Chairman's information was very good indeed as far as it went, but he could not help thinking there hadlbeen some little neglect on the part of some of their officers. He understood from the newa- paper reports that the fire broke out about two o'clock. The officer who discovered it went to the cupboard at the Town Hall to get the hose pipe. As directed, he broke the glass andopened the cupboarddoor,but unfor- tunately there was no fire apparatus in the cupboard. Someone must have been at fault thereto begin with. Instead of the fire brigade being prepared for the work, he was told they had never had a practice. Therefore when they came to couple up their pipes they hardly knew which end to couple Up. Ultimately they did get one coupled up, but there was no water. About an hour and an half elapsed and then a note was received at the Viaduct Works asking them to turn the water on and for the brigade to help to put the fire out. The brigade turned out immediately, but when they arrived at the scene of the fire there was still no water.. The captain of the brigade took it upon himself to send a man to turn the Company's water en, and as soon as the water wa.sturned on,the firemen were enabled to cope with the fire. The Viaduct Brigade' really -.extinguished the fire. and had it not been for them, he believed there would have been a great deal more damage. He thought the Council had made such arrangements as wouldineet any of these requirements,. but as far as this tire was concerned they appeared to - have done nothing at all. He did not know who was responsible, and he was not going to blame anyone, 'but he should say that the chairman of the Fire Brigade Committee was to sonic extent responsible. (Laughter and Hear, hear.) If the instructions of the Chairman of the Water Committee had been carried out,there would have been a water supply when the fire took place. On the Friday night previous to the fire, however, he (Mr. Emmett) received a note to say that .the London and North-Western Rail- way Company's water was not required,and that he was not to turn it on until he heard further. Somebody was to blame for all this. He was told that the Council's fire engine, which perhaps might have been of, some use if it had been in order, was not in working order. What was the use of having a fire engine if it was not in work- ing order when a. fire took place. At this point it was decided that the Clerk should read the minutes of-the meeting of the Fire Brigade Committee that day. The minutes stated that the com- mittee carefully considered the report of the Superin- endent of the Fire Brigade with reference to the recent fire in Market-street, Earlestown. The Deputy Super- intendent (Mr. Hoggard), who was in charge of the brigade at the fire, was in attendance, and was questioned as to the proceedings of the brigade at the fire. It was resolved 'That proper means of telephone communication and of the general equipment of the brigade be left to the Chairman and. Superintendent of the brigade with power to at once order what they consider necesaary." . Mr. Emmett contended that this was a very poor report indeed. It did not touch upon the tire in any shape or form. It only stated what was proposed to be done in future. Dr. Latham, the chairman of the Fire Brigade Com- mittee, said he was always willing to 'accept any res- ponsibility which he considered had fallen upon the right shoulders, but to accept the responsibility for the inefficient condition of the brigade at the present time, he must, considering the length of time he had occupied the position of Chairman of the Committne, decline. That day they had had a report from the Superintendent of the brigade which (lid not bear out altogether the grossly exaggerated reports which had appeared in the press. The committee had gone carefully into the question of the lire. The reports they had had that day distinctly stated that the fire was dis- covered at 2.15 by one of the Council officials. The brigade turned out within seventeen minutes of the first intimation,and when it was considered that it was necessary when the fire was discovered to go down to the gas works to inform the deputy-superintendent, he failed to see that the brigade could have turned out with more alacrity. There was no doubt that the brigade was very far from being in a satisfactory condition—it was very incomplete—but when they remembered that the town- ships had only within the last few months been in posses- sion of an engine, that he himself had been as member of the committee for two months and during that time they had settled the question of the fire engine house, had purchased coasiderable lengths of hosepipe, and tenders had been obtained for other work, he thought it must be admitted that at all events a very great amount of work had been accomplished in a , very short • time. No one regretted the fire more than he did. He should like to say that the committee felt that the thanks of the Council were due to the police and to the Viaduct Brigade for the very excellent work they did. He should like the Clerk to convey an expression of thanks to the men for the very energetic manner in which they turned out. The resolution of the Committee to his mind, showed that the committee were alive not only to their res- ponsibility but to the fact that the work must be done at once. Mr. Emmett said he • was also informed that two horses were taken to the gas works for the purpose of drawing the engine, but when they got to the works there was no harness. Mr. Evans asked whether the engine was ,in working order or not. - •• Mr. Gilmour, who, the Chairman said, was out of order, but who persisted in making some observations., said the engine could not be got out of the Gas Works because coke blocked the way. INFLUENZA INCREASING.

The Influenza Epidemic is again spreading over the Ciuntry. Tnevisitation is of a very prostrating nature, and the disease is certainly on the increase. The origin of the disease or rather the circumstances which bring it about is as mucha mystery as ever. You cannot , trifle with Influenza. It undermines the Nervous System to a greater extent than almost any other disease, and it is Pneumonia that kills, and Pneumonia strikes the man who imagines he has only a mild form of Influenza, and can go about the street just as usual. It can hardly be doubted that the prevalence of Chest and Lung Complaints must be ascribed to the baneful influence of Influenza, whose after-effects so frequently take this form. It was a bad day for this Country when this malignant scourge first visited our shores. Year after year it has never since failed to revive at its due time, and to extort its tribute of human life. Having observed its ravages, and its dreadful effects upon the constitution on the occasion of its former visits, we cannot but regard its recurrence with much fear, as a great many have not yet, if they ever will,regain their former health. As a preventative, we know of nothing equal to Gwilym Evans' Quinine Bitters. A few strong doses taken in time have often proved effectual in ward- ing off attacks of this Malady, and always succeed in mitigating their severity when a patient is under thir iafluence. Do you suffer from Chills, alternating witty Sudden Flushes. Severe Head-ache, accompanied by difficulty of breathing, and symptoms of Catarrh, Pains in the Chest, under and between the shoulders, and a stiffness and soreness of the Muscles, Loss of Appetite and Nervousness. If you suffer from any of these symp- toms, know that they are the forerunners .of Influenza, and it behoves you to re:ort at once to a course of Gwilym Evans' Quinine Bitters. By common consent Patients everywhere admit that they feel more depressed and miser- able after an attack of Influenza than while under its influence, and more than they feel after an attack of any other illness. The best way to banish these miser- able feelings is to take Gwilym Evans' Quinine Bitters, The Vegetable Tonic. We can strongly and confidently recommend this preparation' as it has treated severe cases of Influenza, Heavy Colds, Extreme Weakness, and Langour, with greater success than any other known Remedy. We may also add that as a Remedy for Indi- gestion, Sleeplessness, Low Spirits, Nervousness. and Loss of Appetite, Gwilym Evans' Quinine Bitters stands unequalled. It is sold in bottles 2s. 9d. and 4s. 6d., by all Chemists cud Patent Medicine Vendors, or sent, direct, carriage free, for the above prices, by the sole proprietors: Quinine Bitters Manufacturing Company Limited Llanelly, South Wales. Avoid imitations. See the name " Gwilym Evans" on Label, Stamp, and Bottle.

THE LATE MR. E. KIRKCONNEL. Funeral at Warrington.

The grave closed over the remains of the late Mr. Edward Kirkconnel, of the Mount, Stockton Heath, and head of the firm of Messrs. Davies, Kirkconnel, Davies, Burgess, and Hatton, solicitors, Market-place, at the Warrington Cemetery on Saturdity morning. The coffin containing the remains was brought from Southport to Warrington on Thursday. The service was conducted by the Rev. H. V. Pigot, vicar of St. Thomas's, Stock- ton Heath. The mourners were :—Mr. Kirkconnel (son), Miss Kirkconnel (daughter), Miss Caine, Mr. W. E. Kirkconnel (nephew), Miss M. E. Kirkconnel (niece). Mrs. J. E. Davies (sister-in-law), Mr. Rowland Davies, Miss Bullivant, and Mr. H. Welsby. There were also present: Messrs. Vere B. Davies, John Burgess, and H. Hatton (partners in the firm) ; Messrs. E. WARRINGTON TOWN COUNCIL.

INCREASE OF SALARIES.

The meeting of the Warrington Town Council on Tuesday evening was the quarterly meeting. At this meeting, the proposals for an increase of salaries of Corporation officials are brought up for confirmation and a large number were considered on Tuesday evening. The Mayor (Mr. J. C. Parr) presided, and there were also present: Mr. Alderman Fairclough (Deputy- Mayor), Aldermen W. Bolton, R. W. Francomb, J. S. Green, T. Hutchinson, F. W. Monks, J. Smethurst, T. H. Sutton, Councillors T. Barton, A. Bennett, W. Brough, J. Brophy, J. G. Craik, S. Davies, J. Dodds, James Evans, John Evans, L. Ellison, W. J. Forshaw, T. J. Hulse, J. Lord, A. Peacock, J. Pemberton, J. Roberts, F. R. Roberts, William Roberts, W. Rogers, J. Sampson, D. Tinnion, J. Upson, J. Wilkinson, and the following o'ficials : Messrs. S. Wilson (deputy town clerk), J. Fairhurst (borough treasurer). W. S. Haddock (gas engineer), W. H. Grimsdale (electrical engineer), and J. G. Gornall (medical officer).

THE LATE MR. JOSEPH HARRTSON.

A letter was read by the Deputy Town Clerk from Messrs. it. B. White and Sons, on behalf of Mrs. Harrison, acknowledging the vote of condolence passed by the Council at the meeting held on January 6th. THE POLICE BUILDINGS. THE INSPECTOR'S HOUSE.

The Watch Committee having considered the report of the Borough Surveyor on the completion of the new police buildings, it was resolved that the resident inspector's house be completed, that the old Armoury be taken down, that the wall along Museum street be completed and that the yard be asphalted according to contract; that the BoroughSttr- veyor confer with the architects and report as to the cost of completing the above works and as to the settle- ment to be made -with the contractor ; and that all other matters included in the contract and not carried out be postponed. The MAYOR moved, and Alderman FRANCOMB secon- ded, the confirmation of the minutes. _ _ _

Councillor TINNION said that as one who had consist- ently opposed the planting of the police premises where they were, he should like to know the sum of money likely to be spent on the additioual buildings. They had spent sufficient money on them already. He would probably be correct in saying they had spent too much money and they should hesitate before spending any more.

The MAYOR replied that the whole of the work now being undertaken,was what had already been decided upon; the contract had been actually undertaken. The committee were fully alive to the necessity of spending as little as possible in addition to what hadalready been spent on the premises. (Hear hear.) Con illor TI.N.NroN : What is the approximate cost? The MAYOR replied that the Surveyor was in London and until he returned the information asked for could hardly be gives withany accuracy. Councillor TINNEON' said he would propose an amend- ment that the matter be referred back to the committee for further consideration. • Councillor JAMES EVA-NS seconded.

Alderman SUTTON said he had not been in favour of the whole of the expenditure on the buildings, but it had to be spent,and in time* come itiwould probablyibe found that the course which had been adopted was the best. He was told that Mr. Davenport the builder, would not object if the further building operations were deferred for some time, and that he would not ask for any extras. in that case the committee might see their way to defer the question until they were in a position to give the information which had been asked for.

The MAYOR said he was willing to take that course He thought it was wise to doso under the circumstances (Hear, hear.) This course was agreed to. THE FINANCIAL ADJUSTMENT WITH THE COUNTY COUNCIL.

The Finance and Estate Committee reported that a letter was read from the Local Government Board requesting to know the position of matters respecting the financial adjustment with the county, and the Town Clerk reported that since the date of the receipt of the letter copy of which he had forwarded to the County Council, he had received from the Clerk of the Peace the agreement in duplicate and had executed same on behalf of the Corporation. Alderman SIIAW GREEN moved, and Councillor WILKINSON seconded, the passing of the minutes, and the motion was carried.

A GIFT FROM MESSRS. GREENALL WHITLEY AND COMPANY.

The Paving and Sewerage Committee recommended that the following streets be repaved with second-hand setts : James-street, Naylor-street, Cockhedge-lane Howard-place (corner of Fennel-street and Dial-street) Arpley-walk. The committee also recommended that the salary of Mr. H. P. Brierley be increasod by the sum of EEO per annum to a maximum of £l3O. Councillor TIN:ZION, in moving the confirmation of the minutes, explained that Mr. Brierley was the chief clerk in Mr. Longdin's office and took the responsibility of the accounts, having to debit and credit various departments in the Town Hall. He was a man who had had considerable experience, and they found that he was a very useful servant. Considering that he was still with them—and he had had opportunities of leaving— they thought it only proper to give him a sum equal to the responsibility of the work. In March last Mr. Btierley had another situation offered to him, and the committee pledged themselves to support the recommendation which was now made. The National Telephone Company had applied for permission to take up the footpath along Knutsford-road. So long as they carried out the con- ditions the Committee could not object. He mentioned the matter so that it would be known that it was not the Paving and Sewerage Committee who were again taking up the footpaths.

The GAS bommittea, recommended that the salary of the GAS Engineer (Mr. W. S. Haddock) be increased from _£l3o to 500 a year.

Alderman FAIRCLOISGII in moving the confirmation of this minute, stated that Mr. Haddock was appointed to his present position in July, 1888. at a salary of £3OO per annum. The Canneil would probably remember that prerions to Mr. Haddock's , appointment., Mr. Paterson, the then manager, received a salary of £5OO per annum and was allowed private practice. The duties in con- nection with the management since that date had very considerably increased. For instance, the revenue in 1838 was .234,490 as oompared with £63,931 in 1902. The expenditure was £22,150 as compared with £51,110. The price of gas in 1888 was 3s. per 1,000 feet net as compared with 2s. 7d:now. In 1838 we had 45 "miles of mains as compared with 96 miles. There were 884 public lamps, as compared with 1694; 3,910 consumers, as compared with 10,263 ; 95 workmen were employed as compared with 210 ; the quantity of coal and cannel carbonised was 21,000 tons as compared with 35,000 tons ; the make of gas. was 205,090,000 as compared with 361,000,000 feet; the leak- age of gas—and this was most important—was 15-81, now it was 6.2; the number of gas cookers in use was 124 as compared with 773. Most of these cookers, as the Council were aware, were on the hire system. The profit on the gas undertaking for the past year was about £3,000, notwithstanding that the price for the charge of gas had been reduced, during Mr. Haddock's manage- ment from 3s. to 2s. 7d. per I,oooft. A penny reduction in the price charged for gas meant £l,OOO revenue. Dur- ing the last ten years very extensive and important ex- tensions of the works had been earned out, new works had been built .and the old works abolished, and a tar plant erected, the whole of which had been carried out from plans and specifications, and under the superin- tendence of Mr. Haddock, who had performed his duties with credit to himself and to the entire satis- taction of the committee, who were unanimous in recommending the Council to confirm the increase which they had voted. (Hear, hear.) Alderm uta BOLTON' seconded.

Councillor PEMBERTON, in moving an amendment that no increase be made in the gas engineer's salary, thought considering the high rates in Warrington the Borough debt which stood at £1,100,090 it was not a time to increase salaries. Most people could get along with per week ;In fact there were many respectable tradesmen and manufacturers wile were not getting that income themselves. Councillor LORD seconded the amendment.

ICouncillor WILLIAM Ro-BER--r-s -supported the racom- meadation of the committee, and thought the figures which the Deputy Mayor had given were in themselves a strong recommendation for the increase. What would influence hirajn voting,was the fact that the committee were unanimous in recommending the increase. lAlderman SUTTON said he should also support the !increase to Mr. Haddock, as he thought he was fairly ;deserving of it. • No one should object to a man having I a fair recompense for his services. As they had heard. ; from the Deputy Mayor, the works had increased to a great extent during the past few years. His present • salary was not what Mr. Paterson received when the :works were half the size they ware now. He had heard I it explained by gas engineers and people in other towns 1 that their gas engineer was underpaid—worse paid than any other town of the size of 'Warrington and the magnitude of the works. The fact was that some of the members of the Council objected to an increase for i the sake of objecting, and nothing else. (Laughter.) Councillor Dorms said he was disposed to support , the amendment, not because he objected to a good man having an increase of salary, but because the trade of Ithe town was so depressed that manufacturers could hardly make both ends meet. There was very little employment and a good deal of starvation in the town, and therefore he thought it would be wise to ask for the matter to be deferred for a while until the trade of the town had improved. He believed that Mr. Haddock was a most deserving and capable manager, and whilst endorsing all that Alderman Francomb had said about : him, he ;thought that Mr. Haddock himself would see I that this was not the time to increase the expenditure of the town in view of the condition of things. Councillor TENSION said that unless the Council took the matter up that night and increased the salary, the , matter could net be dealt with again for 12 months. ; The question to be considered was—" Are we getting value for our money? Can Mr. Haddock get £500,2600, jor £7OO elsewhere? Is he Nvorth that money to the Corporation?" This was a business and a manufacturing concern, a successful concern and well-managed, and in dealing with leakages alone,there was a decrease from 1581 to 62. That point showed the worth of the man- agementand that Mr. Haddock was worth the money. At St. Helens the other day the Corporation gave their engineer £BOO a year because he had-an opportunity of going elsewhere, and that money was paid'to keep him in the service of the Corporation. But it was not under I these circumstances that Mr. Haddock came to them for an increase; he applied for an increase not because he thought of getting an appointment elsewhere, but because of his increased value as a gas manager in the gas manufacturing world. He thought the Corporation t were getting good value for the services rendered. 1 Alderman BOLTON contended that Mr. Haddock had a perfect right to expect as much salary as his prede,- . cessor, especially as he was doing double the work the late Mr. Paterson did, and doing it efficiently. He had no doubt Mr. Haddock could get that amount in other , places. Councillor JAMES EVANS said that some of ,the mem- bers of the committee thought this was not an oppor- tune time for increases of salary, when they recognized the depressed condition of things in the town. There were other matters which influenced the committee. • They felt that in Mr. Haddock they had a man conver- sant with the plant from end to end and although they might get a stranger, it was possible that it -would not • be in the best interests of the ratepayers. The amendment was then put to the meeting, and lost, only Councillors Lord; Dodds, and Pemberton voting for it. The MAYOR: There is a large majority against the amendment.

MR. PEMBERTON AND A CONTRACT

Mr. PEMBERTON moved that the recommendation of the Water Committeethat the tender of the Warrington Slate Company Limited to supply the slack required at Winwick Pumping Station for the next three •months at 9s. 4d. per ton delivered be accepted," be referred back. He contended that the tender now proposed for acceptance was not the .lowest. The Engineer was instructed to report which was the best slack for rais- ing steam, and the engineer reported that the Slate Company's tender was -Id. per ton more,but their slack, raised the most steam. He thought each of the four firms tendering ought to have a further opportunity of submitting another tender. Mr. LORDlseconded. Mr. JAMES EVAIsiS inquired whether Mr. Pemberton raised the question in committee. The amendment implied that the committee altered the conditions of the tnider for the purpose of accepting the tender now proposed, and he wished to know if Mr. Pemberton took exception at the time.

, Councillor PEMBERTON said he did. Councillor PEACOCK said he happened to be present at the time the tender was accepted, and Mr. Pemberton did not object to it. • Alderman FRANCOMB explained the course taken by the committee. The two lowest tenders were takenand it was decided that the engineer should give each firm's slack a week's trial. The result of the trial was the recommendation of the committee. Councillor TINNION said the conditions of the tender were not altered at all.

Councillor 44-'ottsHAw said he did not hold a brief for the Slate Company, but he. had a distinct recollec- tion that the company's tender was the lowest. It was a question of the quality of the slack, and in order to give the next lowest tenderer a chance, it was decided that the Engineer should give a week's trial to the slack supplied by the two firms. There was no doubt that the Slate Company's tender was the lowest. Alderman StrrroN remarked that, Councillor Pember- ton seemed to be actuated by a spirit of antipathy to the Warrington Slate Company. On the amendment being put to the meeting, only the proposer and seconder voted for it.

MUSE UM C3MMITTEE.

I THE WORKING OF THE EDUCATION ACT.

At a meeting of the Museum Committee, tenders for construction of a bookcase were examined, and it was resolved that the tender of Messrs. Mounfield and Sons, at £l.B 10s., be accepted. A report of the exhibition of Wild Flowers during the year 1902 was read. It was resolved that Fiides' "Daughter of the Lagoons" be lent to the Corporation of Rochdale ; also that the Technical Instruction Sub-Committee be requested to , report on the 'steps necessary to be taken to put the I Education Act. 1902, in force in Warrington. ' In moving the adoption of the minutes, Alderman Monks referred to the exhibition of wild flowers. He said it was an experiment on the part of the committee, but the results had fully justified the trial. The exhi- bition had been the means of inducing a great number of people, more particularly scholars from the schools and other children, to visit the Museum who otherwise would not have done so. It had also been a means of educating the children in regard to wild flowers. Allud- ing to the working of the Education Act, the Alderman said a scheme had been drawn up and would be submitted to the Council at a special meeting to be held during this month. . _
