THE GYMNASIUM INSTRUCTOR'S SALARY. I Councillor MINION moved the confirmation of the minutes of the Gymnasium Committee which recom- mended that the salary of Mr. 'John L. Kinsley, the gymnastic instructor, be increased from £165 to £lB5 per annum. He pointed out that the committee had not only increased Mr. Kinsley's hours of work but also in- creased the nature of his duties. One reason for this, was the altered condition under which the Education Department insisted upon physical exercises for school children. Councillor Tinnion proceeded to detail the extra amount of work that official had been given to perform. Mr. Kinsley was'engaged to train the school children daily, and as he had no assistance he could not go away for half a day. His hours had been increased by nine or ten per week, and he had well merited an in- crease in his salary. The salary was not a large one for that kind of work as it was a well-known fact that ; gymnasts did not last and did not live very long. Councillor HULSE seconded, and the proposal was approved of. PROGRESS OF THE ELECTRICITY DEPART- MENT. ADVERTISING ON TRAMCARS. The Electricity and Tramways Committee recommen- ded that the tender of Mt. A. C. Burnley, of London, for advertising on the tram cars according to specifica- tion at £23 per average delly running carper annum for five years be accepted. The Committee also recommen- ded that the Town Clerk be instructed to apply to the Local Governmeut for their sanction to the borrowing of £lO,OOO for the purposes of electric lighting. It was I further recommended that the salary of Mr. W. H. ; Grimsdale, the electrical and tramways manager, be increased from £350 to £4OO per annum ; that the salary of Mr. F. V. L. Mathias, chief assistant electrical engineer and tramways manager be increased from £lBO to £2OO per annum; find that the Iwages of the following shift engineers be increased as under :—W. E. Rogers, from £2 to £2 ss. per week; K. F. Bishop, from £2 to £2 ss. per week; G. A. Platt from £1 15s. to .E 1 17s. 6d. per week. Alderman IVIoNKs remarked that the committee were not unanimous with regard to the proposed advertising i on the tramways, but the majority were in favour of the acceptance of this tender. It would mean an income of about £320 a year. Personally he would prefer to sink the money and keep the cars as they were at present, as •he did not think advertisements would add to the appearance, but on the contrary would detract from what had been the very satisfactory appearance of the cars. With regard to the development of the electric i lighting statien, he need not dwell on the estimate . which was submitted by Messrs. Preece and Cardew. It was thought that the proposed extensions would carry ; the work on for about three years. The demand for current for lighting and power purposes was growing rapidly and unless some extensions were put in hand ready for next winter, they might find themselves in rather a serious position. If anything had happened in the way of breakdown during the last few months they had not sufficient spare power to meet the demand,and : some portion of the customers would have been left in the dark and other places wouldhave to be stopped until ' the necessary repairs had been carried en to enable the full service to be given. It was not desirable to run isuch a risk. They had seen the effects of a cheesparing policy at Manchester where the committee ' started well, but fell short in supplying the demand,and Ihad only now been able to overcome the difficulty. In regard to the salaries, he could only say that they kept their expenditure down to the very minimum, and they had reas on to congratulate 'themselves on the success they had attained. As to the advances themselves the committee felt, although they were not unanimous on the matter, that they had been richly earned. The work of the electric light station had grown enor- mously. During the quarter ending December 31st they had generated almost double the number of units of electricity that they generated in the six months ending March 315t,1902,and the income was also double. ' For . the six months ending March 31st, there were generated 191,521 units with an income of £2,239 ss. 9d. and for the last quarter there were generated 327,446 units with an income of £2,452 9s. 6d. The result of the last quarter's running had been to show a net profit of about £4OO, after paying all working expenses and interest and sinking fund charges. They would make a profit of £4OO or rather more during the ensuing quarter,l which would go towards the reduction of the adverse ' balance on their previous working of £2,480. The station had more than reached a profit-paying basis, and he thought justified the proposed extension of the station and the increase in the salaries. Whatever success had been attained was more due to Mr. Grims- dale and Mr. Mathias than to the committee. Alderman FAIRCLOUGH seconded. Councillor F. R. ROBERTS asked whether the advertis- ing contract contained a clause giving the Corporation power to veto any advertisement which they might consider objectionable?

Councillor BENNF.TT moved that the minute with re- gard to the advertising on the tramclars be referred back to the committee. He might not, he said, understand the technicalities of electric cars, but he understood what was pleasing to his eye and to the eyes of other people, and on those grounds he was very strongly averse to the proposal. He contended that it was not a matter of £3OO but a matter of principle. He con- sidered—and he was consistent in this at any rate—that the Corporation ought to set a high standard in matters affecting the wsthetic tastes of the townspeople. They would be going back from

that standard if they adopted the resolution. He for one admired the Warrington tramcars. He had never seen any that had pleased him more. He had seen cars in other towns spoilt by advertisements which were never really artistic. He hoped that in Warrington they would not succumb to the temptation to accept advertisements but would keep the cars in all their pristine beauty. (Laughter.) They had, he believed, some control over the advertisements, but the only way they could really control them so as to make them effective was to leave them in the hands of a corn- ra4tee of artists. (Laughter.) Judging from his experience of the way in which the Corporation had acted in the matter,he did not think there was sufficient artistic taste in the Corporation to secure that the advertisements should not be offensive to people of artistic tastes. There were higher p,rinciples than that of making money, but if that principle was going to dominate to the exclusion of everything else, there were other things which might be done. For instance, they might let the electric lighting station for a gigan- tic hoarding station. (Laughter.) Then they might allow Mr. Beecham or some other advertiser to erect some boards alongside the Park in Kendrick-street. These could be seen from the Cheshire Lines Railway, and the Council could no doubt get a considerable sum for allowing them to be erected. Then, why not make the Town Hall itself into a gigantic hoarding station ? (L mghter.) They would get an immense sum, probably a thousaud a year. A VOICE: Or the police station. Councillor BENNETT : Or the policemen themselves. Why not hire them out. Let them have boards on their backs and chests with "Feet wanted at Brown and Co's "or something of that sort on? (Loud laughter). Continuing, he said the Council must not be actuated purely by the question of money. There was something higher than that. He contended that the advertise- ments would spoil the cars. If the Council were not careful they would be "selling their municipal birth- right for a mess of pottage.' Councillor CRAIK seconded.and thought it would be a great mistake to have advertisements on the cars. He agreed with Councillor Bennett's suggestion as to the Police force. He thought it was a very good one as the force would be of some use then.

Councillor WILLIAM ROBERTS thought it would be a great pity to throw £320 away. He did not see any reason why the advertisements should be of an objec- tionable character or in any way unsightly or disfigur- ing to the cars. They knew their friend Councillor Bennett carried his hobbies to death. If there was any old gravestone of anything of that kind, Councillor Bennett would not mind how objectionable it was to other people so long as it belonged to the ancient days. (Laughter.) He (the speaker) considered that the com- mittee were quite justified in accepting a tender which would bring in a revenue of £320 per annum. Councillor TIN. NroN was of the same opinion and pointed out that only the advertisements approved by the committee could be put on the trams. The adver- tisements would be mainly on the outside of the top of the cars and on the ventilating windows. Councillor PEACOCK asked if tenders were advertised for locally,as he had not seen any advertisement. There might be some enterprising tradesman in Warrington who would take the matter up. The DEPUTY-TOWN CLEnk said tenders were adver- tised for in one local paper. Alderman SUTTON said he was indifferent as to whether the proposal was carried out or not. He sug- gested that advertisements should be put on one car in order that the Council might see whether they were ob- jectionable or not.

Councillor TINNION suggested that a photograph of a car on which were advertisements might be obtained. Alderman MONKS said that all the advertisements would have to be submitted to the committee who could exercise their discretion in the matter.

On a vote being taken, only Alderman Monks, Coun- cillors Bennett, F. R. Roberts, and Craik voted for the amendment, which was therefore lost. Councillor JAMES EVANS moved an amendment that the minute recommending increases in the salaries of Mr. Grimsdale and Mr. Mathias be referred back. He said that when Mr. Grimsdale was appointed tramways manager he (Councillor Evans) took exception to his occupying the dual position of electrical engineer and tramwa-ys manager. It might be urged just now that the business was being worked economically. Perhaps it was. That, however, was not the point. He believed the electrical engineer- ing part of the concern did not merely apply to the electric light station and to the electric light, but to the whole of the electrical engineering, Taking that view of it Mr. Grimsdale's work became rather magni- fied than otherwise. It had been urged that Mr. Grims- dale's appointment was only pro tern. Before there hiid been any attempt to make the position permanent there was an application for a further increase of salary. That was what he took exception to. First of all he thought the committee should consolidate and separate the appointment of electrical engineer and tramways manager:..

Cotincillor TINNEbN : CouMn't do it" and laughter,__ _

Councillor EVANS said he was trying to impress upon the Council that there should be a permanent position and a permanent official. Councillor TINNION : That is different to consolida- tion and separation. Councillor EVANS said he was not going in for pro tems. They had got too many in that Corporation al- ready. (Hear, hoar, and latrghter.) No num could do the work of the two distinct and separate positions effi- ciently unless he had an assistant equal to himself, and while they were building technical schools and urging their young men to become qualified for positions of that kind they should set them an example and make room for them to earn a living when they acquired the ability. (Hear, hear.) He moved that the matter be re- ferred back with the idea of separating the appoint- ments. He believed there were hundreds of men who would jump at the positions at the present salaries.

- - Councillor PEMBERTON seconded. He remarked that it was stated in the Council when the appointment of tramways manager was made,that it was only temporary. He was very much against one official holding two posi- tions. Councillor Pemberton commented upon the fact that Messrs. Preece and Cardew had been entrusted with the work of preparing estimates and specifications for the proposed extensions at the electric lighting station. He said the engines required were only ordinary ones, and the Corporation officials should be capable of pre- piling specifications for them as well as for the mains. Messrs. Preece and Cardew would be paid 5 per cent commission which would amount to 21,000. Alderman SurroN said he was always proud to think that he could say a great deal more in a fewwords, than many people could preach inn long sermon. (Laughter.) It seemed to him that the members of the Connell who proposed these amendmeirts did so without knowingthe whole facts of the case. He had full confidence in the committee and he believed the propositions they had made regarding the increases of salary were justifi- able. He thought Mr. Grimsdale particularly had rendered such valuable service § to*the town as entitled him to the increase, and they would make a great mis- take if they did not consent to it.

Councillor PEACOCK said that allhough he did not object to the salaries,- he supported the amendment because an electrical engineer might be a good one, but that didn't mean he would be a good traffic superintend- ent. He] maintained they were two separate posi- tions, and if Mr. Grimsclale had been such a valu- able servant to the Electricity Committee his time would be better spent in that department ihan in regn- tating the traffic. Councillor F. R. ROBERTS said the permanence or otherwise of the appointment was in RO way affected by the amount of the salary. The question was whether the increase had been earned or not. Could the Corpora- tion get the work as well done for less money ? His opinion was that it could not. He was heattily in favour of the increase apart from the question of per- nianence or otherwise, which was not before the Council. Councillor JAMES EVANS : It is in principle. Councillor SAMPSON asked whether they could get a man to fill the position of tramways managers for £lOO per year, which was the amount added to. Mr. Grims- dales's salary when he received the appointment. Councillor W. ROBERTS said that if there was any institution inaugurated in\S'arrington, the management of which had given satisfaction, it was the tramway. services. If it was for nothing but the excellent selec- tion ot men who were employed by the 6ommittee, that in itself was one of the -strongest recommendations in favour of Mr. Grimsdale's appointment. When they found there were complaints that the work was not being satisfactorily done, then would be the proper time, he thought, to consider the question of a dual appointment. Alderman BOLTON considered £lOO per year was very little for a tramways manager to receive. The succes'S of the undertaking was due in a great measure to Mr Grimsdale, and it was 'better to give Mr. Grimsdale reasonable salary than a gratuity. Mr. Mathias had had A great deal of extra work, simply because Mr. Griinsdale- had had to do other tramway Work; so he was entitled to an increase...

Alderman MONKS said that the appointment to the position of tramways manager was tentative, but since it was made,the Committees had been amalgamated,and experience seemed to have shown that it was desirable that one manager should have control of the electricity station and the tramways. He did not think the Council could have been better served in any way than they had been served by Mr. Grimsdale. He should bs very sorry to see any attempt made to separate the appointments. It was possible that if there were two officials, friction might arise and that would interfere with the efficiency of the service. In reply to Councillor Peacock, Alderman Monks pointed out that Messrs. Preece and Cardew would only receive 21 per cent. commission. The reason it was thought advisable that the firm should prepare the specifications, was that there were so many improvements made in electrical science which followed so quickly one on another, that it was impossible that anyone iq.Mr. Grimsdale's position, occupied as he was to such Iron- siderable extent, to keep in touch with the various developments and improvements. He was sure that the Council got value for money in paying commission. Originally the amount was 5 per cent., hut Messrs. Preece and Cardew had made an exception in this case. The amendment was lost, only Councillors James Evans, Barton, Peacock, Pemberton, and Lord voting for it.

DEPARTMENT.

The Sanitary Works Committee recommenled that o sum of £lO be voted to Richard Wilson, the works manager at Longford Dapst, and that his salary be increased from 435. to 50s. per weak, and th tt the salary of Mr. J. J. A. Parfitt, the S tnitary Works Superinten- dent, be increased from. £l3O to £1.35, with annual increases of £5 to a maximum. of £l5O.

Councillor JOHN E A!..,T 3, in moving the cqnflrmation of the minutes, stated that prior to December 6, 1901, the management of Longford was divided between Mr. Wilson and another man. This scheme did not work very well. and Mr. Wilson was put in charge of the whole works. His wages had not been Increased, and it was now recommended that they be incre:used from 435, to 50s. a week, and that he be given £lO in considera- tion of the tim3 he had had to wait since taking over the management.aWith respect to M. Parlitt's salary the Committee had fixed the maximn:n at £l5O, Mid, there would be an annual increase of £5.

Alderman SMETHURST seconded, and slid the Com- mittee was saving .£2 a week by the arrangement at Longford.

Tiie minutes were passed. MUNICIPAL BOWLING GREEN.. THE STANLEY-STREEr SUN DIAL.

The Town Hall, 'Parks, and Baths Committee reported the receipt of a letter from Mr. Robert Dobsonenclosing a memorial from the inhabitants of Stanley street, pro- testing against the removal of the sun-dial from Stanley- street to Queen's Gardens, and it WAS resolved that the resolution on page 88 of the proceedings relating to the above be rescinded, and that the Borough Surveyor be instructed to have the sun-dial restored to its original position. A letter was read from the Trades and Labour Council askipg the Council to again take into considera- tion the question of providing public bowling greens, and it was resolved that the Town Clerk be instructed to reply to the letter intimating tlytt the committee regret that they cannot at present advise the Council to undertake the provision of public) bowling e 'greens on account of the cost and difficulty of keeping the greens in order.

Alderman BOLTON moved the confirmation of the minutes, and in referring to the letter from Mr. Robert Dobson enclosing a memorial from the inhabit- ants of Stanley-street protesting against the removal of the sun-dial from Stanley-street, to Queen's Gardens, said it seemed to be a sore point that they should have removed it, and so take away the privilege of their examining it whenever the sun shone—which was very rarely in Warrington. (Laughter.) It had now been decided to replace it in Stanley-street. With regard to municipal bowling greens,that was a difficult matter. It was one which the committee would like to carry out so far as the public were concerned. They had not, who- ever,a plot okrass in the town which would stand tramp- ling upon. Vilraichever way they weat,they had found that there was smoke coming from some foundry or works NVixich prevented the roots taking action. They thought the proposed expenditure on bowling greens would be a waste of money. Alderman SNIETITURST seconded the adoption of the minutes.

Councillor LtMs Ev ANS moved an amendment to the minutes. He had on one or two occasions proposed the introduction of municipal bowling greens to the Coun- cil. He had heard some very weak aeguments against thorn in tirnoi gone by, bat, perhap3 he had heard the weakest that night. Alderman Bolton knew, and knew full well, that there was grass suitable for a bowling green within 25 yards from that Council kiChamber. There was not a bowling green within three milesof Warrington which was equal to the lawn behind the Town Hall.

Alderman BOLTON: It is not grass, it is moss. (Laughter.) Councillor JAMES EVANS repeated that there was no grass which was equal to it. Alderman Bouros : it is-nearly all moss. douncillor JAMES EVANS: Very Well, Mr. Moss. (Laughter). Oontinning he said, that when .he intro- duced the matter on-the last occa,sion,he Suggested the ground behind Bank House, and the main argument used against the proposal,was that it was not a suitable place for a bowling green. Alderman Heaton stated at the time that when they adopted a bowling green they would not put it in a back yard. He (Councillor Evans) was not merely an advocate for the greens because of the game itself, but there were other social and moral reasons. It was well known that the game of bowls was now more attractive than it had ever been in the history of the country; in fact, they had heard it urged just lately that the magistrates should transfer a licence because there would be the attraction of a bowling green. (Laughter). Why his friend Alderman Bolton should onpase bowling greens he was at a loss to under- stand. The Corporation tried a great deal more costly and less useful experiments than municipal bowling greens. (Hear, hear). He said on the last occasion that they had got suitable land, in fact it had been a question in that Council as to what they should do with the land in Springfield, and when he suggested that they should utilize it for a bowling green somebody said it was net bought for the purpose, and it was urged that it was not a suitable locality. He knew no reason why it was not. He was met in Bold-street, when they were about to lay out the Queen's Gardens, by a lady, who expressed surprise that the Corporation should bring down "the roughs" into Palmyra-square. (Laughter). He thought Queen's Gardens had "made" Palmyra-square. (Hear, hear.) Springfield was quite worth trying for the pur- pose of a bowling green,and why the Corporation should object to the experiment, costing £2OO or £3OO, which would lay out a good bowling green in Springfield, he did not know. (Hear, hear,) He did not like to attribute motives to people, but the opposition came in such a way that he was bound to say that there were motives. While he did not patronize the greens at the Liberal or the Conservative Club, or any other greens, in the inter- ests of those who did,he thought the public ought to have their ambition and desire for public greens grati- fied. He moved an amendment that the minute be referred back with definite instructions from the Council that they should not merely entertain the subject, but that they consider the advisability of establishing a green at Springfield. (Hear, hear.) The IVIA.Yoa said he was sure they all felt that the proiect might be accomplished. Alderman BOLTON said Councillor James Evans, had lost sight of one thing with regard to Queen's Gardens. They could not possibly utilize the gardens in a better way than they were utilized now, and as gardens they served ten times more people than they would as bowl- ing greens. (Hear, hear.) The bowling greens could only be used in summer, whereas the gardens were used both summer and winter. (Hear. hear.) . Councillor CRAIK pointed out that Councillor James Evans had never said anything about converting the Queen's Gardens into bowling greens. Councillor EVANS : No, I never even suggested it. I was referring to the ground known as Springfield. The MAYOR suggested that the Chairman of the Corn- mitteebe asked if he would agree to the minute being sent back again, with the' suggestion that a bowling green should be made in some part of the town. Alderman Bouros: It is a matter of cost. If the Council wanted greens they would have to 'include in their next estimate arate of Id. or 2d. inthe E. ("No no.") They would want in the estimate about £2,000. (A Voice: "Not near that amount.") Councillor EVANS: Nonsense. Alderman BOLTON then agreed to the Mayor's sugges- tion, and the other minutes were confirmed. ALTERATIONS AT THE CEMETERY. •

THE PROPOSED WIDENING OF MANCHESTER ROAD.

After visiting the chapels in the Cemetery, the Cemetery committee recommend that the urns on the south side of the Church of England and Nonconformist Chapels be removed to the north side. This is owing to some evil disposed person having thrown down the urns, thus breaking them. With respect to the Nonconformist Chapel, owing to complaints having been received in reference to insufficiency of light at the reading desk, the committee recommend that a portion of the screen be removed, and a rod and curtain fixed so that the clergy may r.be. This will allow the desk being placed nearer the north window, thereby increasing the light on the desk. ST. JAMES' GROUND

The MAYOR moved :—That the Bye-laws made by the Town Council at the meeting held on the 2nd December, 1902, for the regulation of the St. James' Ground (formerly St. James' Old Burial Ground)be confirmed. The motion was agreed to and the Council rose. A report of the discussion on the proposals for the core" pletion of the Technical School appears in another column.

WORKINGMEN'S MISSION, BANK QUAY. Congregational Tea Meeting. Thewlis-street schoolroom was the scene of the =anal congregational tea meeting in connection with the Workingmen's Mission on Monday evening. About 180 sat down to tea. The tables were presided at by the following ladies :—Mesdames Ashley, T. Dale, G. DeLeY D. Hill, T. Taylor, Henry Edwards, F. Atkinson, Johnson, Millington, Woods, Misses E. Tilling, Walling" ton, Hough and Evans. The subsequent proceedings were presided over hY Captain George Crosfield, and he was accompanied on the platform by Mrs. Crosfield, Miss Crosfield, Mrs' Roome and Mr. N. Hough (president). THE ANNUAL REPORT. The Hon. Secretary (Mr. H. Edwards), read tho, annual report which stated that the year 1902 woulo bear favourable comparison with any of its predecessorg in the history of the mission. The church raembershi? had increased, and the services in the chapel, and the, various organizations were very well sustained, aeo much good had been accomplished. The pulpit had been well supplied Sunday by Sunday by men whoa they had felt had preached the gospel for Christ's sake' Death had dealt very kindly with them, for not ono single member of the congregation had passed aWaY •during the year. The committee had to record the resignation of the choir master, Mr. Hassid!, whose work had taken him out of town. Th°. committee wished to tender him their sincere than for past services and to wish him ever success in his new sphere of labour. Mr. F. Atkinson had very kindit\ promised to undertake the duties of choirmaster Oa organist. It was not often that the committee had to ask the members to make a special effort to assist tho mission financially, but they had had to do so during the, past year, so that the ehapel might be cleaned anl beautified. This work had now been accomplished aoli the committee tendered their thanks to those who assisted. The open air services were still continun• during the summer months and were the means doing much good. The poor fund was tlir paused by Mr. Thomas , Dale, who was 0310 to give temporary relief as well as spiritual comfort- There was a balance in hand in connection with Olt fund of £2 18s. Old. In regard to the Mission, the totax income was £74 7s. 11d., and the expenditure £72 6s. 11d., leaving a balance in hand of £2 -Is. Tho3r trusted in the coming years to be a further blessing to the neighbourhood in which the Mission was situated; They were thankful that the past year had witnessed the close of the war in South Africa, and they hoped that the peace and goodwill now established might be of u. permanent character. The Committee of management for the year consisted of the following :—President. Mr. N. Hough; vice-president, Mr. T, Dale; treasurer, Mr Taylor; secretary, Mr. H# Edwards; committee, Messrs. T. Ashley, G. Dale, D. Hill, W.- Woolley, A Tilling, - Atkinson, W. Worrall. The CHAIRMAN congratulated the secretary on the report he had presented, which he (the chairman) con- sideredan admirable one. He was very sOrry that, hie' brother and Mr. Roberts were unable to be present, a. 5 they were away on business. As they were doubtleso aware, Mrs. Crosfield was shortly to leave the neigh- bourhood, but he wanted to assure them that although Mrs. Crosfield was leaving the district she would not take any less interest in the school and mission, and , wouldialways be gladlto come down at any time they might wish to see her. There was only one point in the report to ." which he would refer; that was with reference to giving relief to the poor. He daresay some of them were aware that there was a discussion in the news- papers at the present time with reference to charity, and charity giving, and it only illustrated the great care that needed to be exercised in the giving of charity -- (Hear, hear). An excellent musical programme was submitted. Miss Crosfield sang "Awake" (Pelissier) and also con- tributed a pianoforte solo for which she was encored. Mr.E. Pownall's robust bass voice was heard to great advantage in the songs "The Sound of the Drum' and " Trankadillo," and Miss Hayward was very successful in her interpretation of the songs "May Morning" anc: The Enchantress." Mr. J. Corrin (tenor) also secureo a hearty reception for his singing of" Star of the Desert," and "The Pilgrim of Love." A quintetto orchestra of violins and mandolines contributed selec- tions. The performers were—Misses E. and A. Ward, G. Holden, A. Crompton and L. Turton, and they per- formed with such taste and ability as to secure ail encore. The humour of the evening was supplied bY Mr. R. Sumner, junior. Mr. Nelson was the accom- panist. Mr. 1:)1,, in the course of a few remarks, alluded to the impending removal of Mrs. and Miss Cmusuflelidufrtohino Warrington, and stated • that there were y who town besides those cOnnected with that mission,. Crosfield family in opening Walton Lea_t the public for miss them. He alluded to the generooshity btlhioe saidfo thethe of the eaut sustainti I tafih ao twleomss there,iu tahni dst, respect. He was glad to heafou the interes of the family the y at Bank Quay, mission late Mr. Crosfield, which was instituitned reached dhetrhee wash high ideal o would be maintained. (Hear, lhdeabre.)seeFnrothmitththeeyrespotir which had been read,it would still much i tmarked done, for progress haety thehitd not Miss on. ear the founder, but they hoped in time to make the Mission a blessing to every householder in the neighbourhood- The question had often been asked " Why don't men go to church?" He Svas afraid that the freedom connected with public worship had had something to do with the indifference with which some people regarded it. lie pleaded fora larger congregation at the morning servise and thanked the parents for the regularity with which they sent their children to the Sunday school. Mr. ASHLEY proposed a vote of thanks to the Chair- man, Mrs. Crosfield and Miss Crosfield, and to all vvho had assisted in the programme. He remarked that the Crosfield family had been generous contributors to the mission for the past 40 years and the mission and schools would be an everlasting memento to the late Mr. Cros- fiele. "OLD BOYS" AT DINNER.

Grammar School Re-Union. Mr. F. Monks and the Non. Trading Agitation.

The members of the coinmittee which arranged the first annual dinner in connection with the Warrington Boteler Grammar School Old Boys' Union must have been gratified and encouraged by the success attend- ing the function which took place at the Lion Hotel on Thursday last week. There was a large gathering of " old boys," the chair being occupied by Mr. C. S. Burgess in the unavoidable absence of the President (Mr. John White.) A- capital dinner was served, the catering being .very satisfactorily carried out by Mrs. Gilbert. After dinner a short toast lit was gone through, and the speeches were interspersed bi- excel- lent musical items, including pianoforte solos by Messrs. S. Woods and W. H. Payton; songs by Messrs. W. Owen, W. • J. Forshaw, B. S. Newcombe and Captain Fairclough. The arrangements had been ably carried out by a committee, Mr. J. H. Smethurst discharging the secretarial duties. He has, indeed, been the prime mover in this re-union, and deserves mach of the credit for its success. The usual loyal toasts haying been honoured, the company drank in silence "The pious memory of Sir Thomas Boteler," the founder of the school.

, THE FORMATION AND OBJECTS OF THE UNION. Before proceeding further with the toast list the Chairman made a few general observations. With regard to the formation of the Union, he would remind them that it originated in a letter which appeared in the local papers asking any old boys who were desirous of forming such a union to communicate with Mr. J. H. Smethurst. Several replies were received to that letter and a committee was formed out of those who replied to consider what should be done to establish the pro- posed union. After several meetings, the objects and rules of the union were drawn up and he claimed for them that they had been drafted with considerable care and in a form which would form the basis of a perman- ent and successful union. The speaker then referred to the rules of the union. They would "see that it was to be governed by a committee consisting of twelve old boys and that the officers were to be elect- ed annually. The first committee consisted only of nine old boys representing old boys of different periods during iihe last 30 years, but it was their intention at the next re-union to elect to the com- mittee three old boys who by their presence there that night had shown their sympathy with and interest in the movement. (Applause.) In a few years the elder members of the committee wonld resign but only in such numbers as to preserve a unity of action anda con- tinuity of policy. A book of record would be kept and would contain an account of the proceedings at the annual dinner and also the signatures of those old boys who were present at the dinner. In order that that book might be preserved, even if the union were to be dissolved, the committee had decided to hand it over to the custody of the governors of the Grammar School, and in doing so, he thought the committee had acted wisely. (Applause.) As regarded the objects of the union,as they all knew,the original idea was the re-union of old boys at an annual dinner, but not only that the Committee hoped that the Union would be the means of encouraging and maintaining a feeling of esprit Jr corps amongst the grammar school boys both past and present. He hoped that the other two objeets would meet with their approbation, and the Committee trusted that all who had been associated with the school would do their best to assist in promoting those objects. There should be no difficulty in carrying out the first object, which was the provision of a sum each year which would be sufficient to establish a prize for hiStory, of the value of about three guineas, to be open for com- petition to the whole school and to be especiallyassocia- ted with the old boys. (Applause.) With regard to the s'scond, that of founding a university exhibition for the school, it would of course be much more difficult to carry it out, but he hoped that. the proposal would not prove impracticable, and that it would lw accomplished within a reasonable length of time. (Applause). They could not expect to raise the whole of the £l,OOO which would be required at the present moment, or even in the near future, but he hoped that all of them would contribute towards the funds, which, once started, he was sure would go on. Some of them had no doubt heard adverse criticisms of the lines upon which the first committee had worked. He did not in- tend to reply to those criticisms, as time alone would show whether the committee had acted on the right lines or not. (Applause). He would let it suffice to say that it was the desire of the committee in forming the union to foster a spirit of good feeling amongst those who from time to time had been members of the old school, which had so. many happy recollections for them, and to do all they could to promote the prosper- ity and the welfare of one of the most valuable insti- tutions in the town. (Applause).

LETTERS OF APOLOGY

Mr. J. H. SMETHURST then read letters of apology for non-attendance from Mr. John White, Mr. Robert Pierpoint M.P., Mr. J. C. Parr, the Revs. Canon Willis, R. Greenall and J. Hannah. and Messrs,E. R. Pickmere (town clerk of Liverpool),W. J. Burgess,E. A. Edelsten; W. Spinks, J. C. Bate, J. Edelsten, P. Wilson, C. Hutchings, F. C. Herzog, V. B. Davies, and A. Sharrock, all of whom expressed deep regret at their inability to be present at the first annual dinner.

===== 0002601_19030206_art0110.txt | included=true | included =====
"PROSPERITY TO THE SCHOOL."

Mr. l. A. WHITE proposed" Prosperity to the school" and in doing said he thought that the toast should have 1,,,0n that of wishing continued prosperity to the school for as far as his recollection went the school was pros- perous 30 years ago, and he believed that it was pros- nerous still. (Applause.) He coupled with the toast thv. names of the masters, both past and present. The toast was enthusiastically honoured.

The Rey. E. J. Wir,LcOCKS briefly responded and said he believed the standard of proficiency in the school had been maintained and he hoped that it would be wintained in the future. (Applause.). :It was the aim of his colleagues ands-himself to give the highest pos- sible general culture to all the boys end to fit them for their individual careers. Their - old boys were to be found in all the different walks of life and many of them were public men of repute. (Applause.) Now that the Education Bill had become law, it was a question as to what position they would occupy in any ystem. As a recognised public school, they could nOt be disre- garded in any scheme which might be devised for the benefit of educqtion in the town. Tie thought that re-union of old boys would serve as a strength and. en- couragement to those who had at heart the true interests of the higher education in the town—(applanse)--and he believed that one of the most important effects of that meeting would be the supportthat would thereby he given to help on the school. The speaker then made several suggestions as to altering the rules of the union, and - in conclusion expressed the hope that the present would be, only the first of a series of very happy re-unions. (A ppla use.) _ _ _

The Rev. a. SMITH and the _Rev. G. HOLT.INWELT,, former masters at the Grammar School, also returned thanks. MR. F. MONKS AND THE COUNCIL CRITICS.

Mr. F. MoNEs in submitting the toast of "Prosperity to the County Borough of Warrington," said he thought there was a meetin'T being held somewhere that night —(laughter—to criticise the members of their Town Council. He supposed that some of the Councillors would get a lashing. some of them might deserve it, but he thought it was rather unfair and un-English for th won-traders to take the stand which they had taken , by asking that the Council should not only grant them facilities to enable them, if they could, to prove the charges which they had made—and those not stated charges—against the members of the Corporation, but that they should also allow them to act as judge and jurv.fle hoped that the TownlCotmcil would keep to the course they had decided upon, and that they would con- tinue" to hold to the resolution which they had nassed. He was quite satisfied, that the proposed inquiry would not prove anything like all the charges which had been made and which he thought had been recklessly made. (Applause.) The lgitators proposed making a • great change. They were going to turn off the Council their friend Alderman Sntton and the rest of the Councillors—(laughter and Alderman Sutton: "They are. not going to' turn me off") —and were cong to pitch the man in the street into thr, COMICH to look after their interests. Mr. linnks then referred to the Education Act, in ,-I,:ch he said they were all more or less interested. He I,•lieved ;n accepting the Act and making the best of it, oiouall no doubt some of them did not think it. was the 11,0 1-1-Ipv could get. bat if they waited until they got the -Pry best they would have to wait a long time. The Examiner, Friday, February 6, 1903.

ALDHRM AN SUTTON AND THE INQUIRY

The toast having been honoured, Alderman SUTTON responded, and said the 'toast was one which he considered every loyal inhabitant of the town should support and drink most heartily, inasmuch as in wishing prosperity to the County Borough they implied nothing less than a wish for the welfare and happiness of every resident in the borough. Warrington, he was proud te say, had held its own for many years in regard to its industries, some of which had made it famous almost all over the world. (Applause). During the past 20 years, the tone and moral and social condi- tions of the inhabitants of the borough had been materially improved in ivny ways. They were going to be pat to a great expense Mover the inquiry which was to be held, and all they would get to know as a result of the inquiry would be just as much as was known at the present moment. The Council had given all the information they possibly could give, and had told everything they knew. and yet they were going to be put to the expense of an inquiry, which might be cause of an increase in the rates of a penny or twopence in the 2 just to "ascertain what they already knew."

===== 0002601_19030206_art0114.txt | included=true | included =====
OTHER TOASTS,

Mr. HENRY GREEN-A-LL— proposed the health of the governors of the Gramniar School, coupling with it the name of Sir Gilbert Greenall. The toast was received with musical honours, and Sir GILBERT briefly responded. Mr. DOBSON proposed the health of their Chairman. Mr. Burgess presided over, the gathering as a substi- tute, and he (the speaker) hoped that it would not be long before he was able to take the place of Chairman by reason of his being president of the union. (Applause.) The toast having been honoured. Mr. BURGESS in responding proposed a vote of thanks to the artists for their services. _ . .

The vote having been heartily accorded, the singing of "Auld Lang Syne " concluded a most enjoyable evening. During the evening a collection was taken in aid of the projected scheme for founding a scholarship to be competed for by boys attending the Grammar School, the sum of £6 10s. being realised.

LETTERS TO THE EDITOR.IMARRIAGE OF MISS PARR.

NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS.

Authors of letters intended for publication must in all cases send their names and addresses to the Editor as a guarantee of good faith. Matter intended for publicatiom must be written on one side only of the paper and sent in, if possible,not later than Thursday. Correspondents are strongly advised to write briefly. A short letter has a much greater chance of insertion than a long one. We cannot under ordinary circumstances undertake to return the manuscripts which are not used.

" DARDIE," RUNCORN AND "lONA," WARRINGTON.— Your letters hardly seem of sufficient general interest, and are too long, for publication in our columns.— ED. "Ex."

The Unionist Meeting.

An Apology From Mr. Joynson-Hicks.

Sir,—My attention has been called to the leaderette in your issue of the 31st January and to the letter of Mr. Parker who is, T believe, the President of the War- rington Liberal Association, in both of which fault is found, and I think rightly, with a remark used by myself expressing my fervent thankfulnessthat I did not know Mr. Crosfteld. _

I should him" thought it would have been hardly necessary for me to say that this remark was used en- tirely in apolitical sense and did not in any way refer- to thepersonal position or character of Mr. Crostield whom I believe to be a gentleman of unimpeachable in, tegrity, an excellent employer of labour, and one who, apart from his political views, I should esteem it an honour to meet; so far, then, let, me frankly apologise if what I said has injured the feelings of any of his friends.

Having said this much lam tempted, though natur- ally I have not either the time or the inclination to enter into a correspondence upon all criticisms upon my political speeches, to emphasize my reasons for thank- fulness that I have no part or lot in Mr. Crosfield's political views. Before the meeting. I was furnished with copies of some of Mr. Crosfield's recent speeches from which it appears that he is what in ordinary language is termed "a pro-Boer," that is to say, one who believes that the Boers were 'right and the English were wrong, and that the war was the direct result of what he is pleased to call Mr. Chamberlain's 'evil diplomacy." If, Sir, there is one fault which I possess to an exaggerated degree it is. a fervent patriotism, and rightly or wrongly I would support my country against allkomers, but fortunately, not only the whole of the Unionist Party, but, I honestly believe, the bulk of the thinking portion of the Liberal Party, headed by Sir Henry Fowler, Sir Edward Grey, Mr. Haldane, and Mr. Asquith, agree that the war was both a just and a necessary one. You can perhaps therefore imagine, though you may not sympathise with, my feelings on hearing what is nothing more or less than a veiled charge of wholesale murder hurled at the head of the greatest Colonial Minister this country has ever had by a man who hopes to occupy a Lancashire seat in Parliament. I need hardly say that I have not the least intention of dealing with Mr. Parker's personal abuse, which only shows that my angwer to Lord Rosebery, founded upon thehistory of the past twenty years, has hit him ex- tremely bard. This only I will say, that my charge of ..cowardice against the Liberal Government of 1881 was fully meant, was, and is, thoroughly believed in by the great mass of the nation, and is moreover borne out by the speech of Lord Kimberley, a member of the then Cabinet, which he made at Newcastle on the 14th of November 1889.

Might I call Mr. Parker's attentiOn to the following quotation from that speech:— "So far as he (Lord Kimberley) was concerned, and to a great extent Mr. Gladstone, their reason was not, as some people thought, a mere sentimental reason. They found themselves in this position: the Free State was then very friendly. There was at its head President Brand—as good a friend of ours as any man in South Africa,—Presi- dent Brand used his utmost influence with the people of the Free State to keep them back from joining the Transvaal Republic in the event of the war going on. At last Mr. Brand sent a message in which he said he had done his utmost, that he had gone to the end of his tetherand could not hold his burghers in any more, and that if England went On she would have the Free State against her. More than that the Government had plain indications from the Cape Colony itself that there was sympathy there which might give rise to an ex- treme and serious difficulty; and the conclusion they came to, whether it was wise or not, was that, painful to the last degree as it was for them to make peace under such circumstances, they were taking the right course to avert the calamity they saw impending." In 1899 Lord Salisbury and Mr. Chamberlain had the Transvaal against them, had the Orange Free State against them, had a serious rebellion in Cape Colony, and yet they showed no cowardice but went forward with Great Britain at their back, and the country showed in 1900, and still shows to-day, that their confidence is with the Government which believes in the Imperial Mission of Great Britain rather than with the factions of a mutilated party which has ceased even to believe in itself. I have the honour to be yours S:c..

W.-.1-6-fR-so-:V HICKS.

A WORKS' ACCIDENT. Warrington Man's Claim For Compensation.

William Salt, 11, Mill-lane, brought an action against 'the St. Helens Cable Company, at the Warrington County Court, on Thursday to recover £202 for the loss of four fingers from his right hand whilst followinr, his employment at the company's works on August 2:37d. Mr. Cuthbert Smith, barrister (instructed by Mr. Steel) appeared for the applicant and Mr. Browne represented the respondents. Mr. Smith said the grounds on which applicant sought to recover,were that the place on which he had to stand whilst working at machinery was insecure and did not afford him a firm foothold, and also that the machinery was dangerous and not properly fenced. Applicant had originally been employed as a roll turner but ultimately entered the employ of the St. klelens Cable Company. Part of his duties was to attend to a machine called a warmer up. The machine consisted of two large cylinders and Salt, had to pass long strips of rubber through them. On the date of the accident he was standing on two planks which had been placed on the floor for him to stand on, beanuse the concrete under- neath was broken and in a bad state. He was putting rubber between the cylinders when the boards, which were insecure and had always been insecure and of which he had complained, tilted, and he was thrown forward, his hand going straight between the rollers. The consequences were that half of the hand had to be amputated. Before the accident,Major Rowe, inspector of Factories, visited the works, and one witness would say that he heard the Major advise that a bar be placed across the top of the machine to prevent anyone falling forward. No bar had, however, been placed there and another man he should call was caught in the machinery some time after the present accident, his shirt being torn off. Major Rowe was called and said he regarded the warmers up as being dangerous. He couldnot remember whether he made the suggestion as to the bar before the accident, but it was very probable that he did. By Mr. Browne: The company had not been summoned by him. The workmen said they could not work if such a bar as he suggested were placed before the machines, and it would be a great responsibility for him to say that the suggestion must becarried out. Applicant gave evidence bearing out Mr. Smith's statement, and said he had complained of the state of the planks to a foreman. Evidence was also given by William Allcock, a boy named Bellfield, John Burne, and Wiilliam Salt, jun., as to how the accident occurred and the unsteadiness of the boards. The last witness said that his shirt was caught in the machine some days after the accident to his father.

The applicant's case had just concluded, when the hearing was adjourned until Thursday next.

IMPUDENT THEFT AT EARLESTOWN.

At Earlestown -Occasional Court, before Mr. J. W. Emmett, Harry Brooks, late of Southport, but now residing in Wargrave-road, Earlestown, was charged with stealing an overcoat on Saturday evening, the property pf Mr. E. T. Evans, of Market-street, Earlestown. Prisoner was seen taking the overcoat from outside prosecutor's shop. He made three attempts to pull the coat off the nail on which it was hung, and the third time he succeeded in getting it down. He madea hasty retreat into a side street, but was followed by one of prosecutor's assistants. When he was eventually given in charge he was wearing the overcoat. Whilst P.C. Mellor was taking him to the Police Station he became very violent. Prisoner was the worse for clVink•—He was remanded to Warrington County Petty Sessionß, and on being brought up on Wednesday was fined 10s. and costs.

INTERESTING SCENE AT GRAPPENHALL

Few marriages -in the district have ,excited such keen • interest as that which was solemnized in St. Winifred's Church, Grappenhall on Tuesday, when the weddine, took place of Miss Katherine Agnes Parr, eldest (laughter of the Mayor and Mayoress (Hr. and Hrs. Joseph Charlton Parr) of Grappenhall Reyes and Staunton Park, Herefordshire, to Sir Archer Croft, Bart, of Lugwardine Court, Hereford, son of the late Sir Herbert Croft, Bart., one of the Government in- spectors of constabulary. When the announcement of the engagement was Made hi Christmas week it came as a pleasant surprise to the many Warrington friends of the Mayor and Mayoress, and • Miss Parr. By her winning disposi- tion and cordial assistance in several local functions during her father's Mayoralty, Miss Parr has won the hearts of many of the townspeople, and when her engagement became known she was warmly congratulated. Sir Archer Croft, Bart., made two public appearances in Warrington after the, publica- tion of the engagement, namely, at the Infirmary Ball and the Unionist Demonstration in the Parr Hall. He is a member of an old and distinguished Hereford funily, the baronetage dating hack to the year 1671. The present baronet was born in September, 1868, and succeeded to the title on the death of his father last year.

- A wish was expressed that the marriage should be a quiet one, but. nevertheless a large congregation was attracted to the church to witness the ceremony. The picturesque village • was ths ; scene of an animated spectacle, and a liberal display of bunting lent additional colour to the scene. Opposite the house iff Mr. Lister-Kaye, in Broad-lane, a triumphal arch of evergreens, sur- mounted with flags and some pretty bunting had been erected. A motto was displayed in the centre. On one side were the words " Long life, health, and happiness " in red letters on a white ~round, and on the other side "God bless the bride and bridegroom." Lines of flags stretched across .the .road leading to the church and flags were displayed at s.?veral of the principal houses in. the2village „in honour of the oc3a- sion. An awning had been erected from the church- yard gate to the west door of the church, the ground being covered with red baize, and this covered passage was reserved for the wedding party and guests. A beautiful collection of arum lilies had been intro- duced into the church fron the conservatories at Grappenhall Reyes and looked exceedingly pretty. Shortly before the appointed time, the guests com- menced to arrive, and meanwhile the organist (Mr. Asquitii Wood) played several appropriate selections on the organ. The arrival of the bride was notified by the playing of Loheagrin's " Bridal March." She walked down the aisle, leaning on the arm of her brother, Mr. Roger Parr, and was attended by her two sisters as bridesmaids. She was met at the altar rails by the bridegroom, and they immediately took up their positions before the officiating clergymen, Rev. Herbert Harington, PeterstOw Rectory, Ross, Herefordshire, and the Rev. Richard Creenall, Rector. of Grappen- hall. The service was fully choral and the singing was led by a full snrpliced choir. When the cere- mony commenced, the scene inside the church was a brilliant one.. As the sun fitfully glinted through the stained glass windows of the venerable church on to the living wealth of beauty below, the etisqn* was extremely pleasing. _ _ _ _. _

The bCid-e who was given away by her father, looked charming in a dress of white satin trimmed with tulle and chiffon, and court train. She wore a wreath of orange blossom and veil, and carried a beautiful bouquet of white orchids fashioned by Mr. Masterton, the head gardener at Grappenhall Heyes. Her brides- maids were her two sisters, Miss Elinor Parr and Miss Margaret Parr, and they wore white Cloth dresses, trimmed with beaver, and muffs to match, supplied by Messrs Hodgkinson and Co.,Ltd, Bridge-street,and hats supplied by Louise, of Regent-street,. London. Each carried a very pretty bouquet. The duties of best man were satisfactorily discharged by Mr. Owen Croft (brother of the bridegroom). Amongst those present were :—The Mayor and Mayoress, the Misses Parr, Mr. Roger Parr, •Mr. and Mrs. C. W. Lister-Kaye (Worksop), Mr. and Mrs. Alan Lister-Kaye; and Miss Kaye, Mr. and Mrs. Archer Greathed (sister and brother-in-law of the bride- groom), Mrs. Cecil Parr; Mr. and Mrs. Usher - (Great Bndworth), Mr. and Miss Worrall (High Legli), Mrs. Frost, Mrs. Lister (Agden Hall), Mr. and Mrs. John Afaclver, ths Rector of Warrington, and Mrs. Willis, • Miss Willis, Mrs. Pickmere, Mr. and Mrs. John Goxon. and members of the Warrington Town Conned with their wives and lady friends. - The hymn " Lead us Heavenly Father, lead us " was simg, and at the close of the ceremony the happy couple left for the vestry, accompanied by several of their relatives and more intimate friends. When they emerged and walked down the aisle the organist played Mendelssohn's well-known " Weddinr, March." On leaving the doors of the church, the bells rang out a merry peal. The large number of spectators who witnessed their departure ,refrained from throwing rice and confetti by request, but they freely expressed their hearty good wishes for the happiness of th 3 bride and bridegroom. There was no formal reception at Grappenhall Heyes, and the invited guests to the hall were limitea to Mr. and Mrs. C. W. T,ister-Kaye, Mr. and Mrs. Alan Lister-Kaye, Mr. and Mrs. Archer Greathed, and Mrs Cecil Parr. Later in the afternoon Sir Archer and hi bride left for a tour in Devonshire, Lady Croft's travelling dress being composed • of blue cloth with jacket and hat to match. .Prior to their departure, the bride and bridegroom and the guests were photo- graphed by Messrs. Birtles, of Sankey-street.

THE PRESENT FROM THE WARRINGTON TOWN COUNCIL.

INTERESTING CEREMONY AT GRAPPENHALL

HEVES.

As will be seen from the list of presents, the gift of the Warrington Town Council was a solid silver claret jug. This VMS supplied by Messrs. Eustance and Com- pany and was of Florentine design in bas relief with solid silver strap work running round the body. It bears the borough arms enamelled on a silver shield in relief with the crest of the Croft family superimposed. The jug is enclosed in a beautiful morocco ease with a silver shield on the outside bearing the inscription : " Presented to Miss K. A. Parr by members of the Warrington Town Council on the occasion of her marriage with Sir Archer Croft, Bart., February 3, 1903." _ _ .

, ; The presentation was made in the library at Grappen- hall Heyes on Monday afternoon by Aldermen Sutton and Fakirclough, on behalf of the Corporation, who were accompanied by Mr. Stanley Wilson (deputy town clerk.) Thera were also present the Mayor and Mitycress, Miss Parr, Mr: Roger Parr, and Sir Archer Croft.

..... . Alderman SUTTON, addressing Miss Parr, said that when it was announced that a marriage had been arranged between her and Sir Archer. the members of the Town Council thought it would afford them a fitting opportunity to gracefully show their respect for their

esteemed Mayor and his family by presenting a small monlento of the happy and auspic- ious event. The Deputy-Mayor and himself enjoyed the honour of having been deputed to ask Miss Parr's acceptance of it,and to wish her much happiness. Miss PARR gracefully received the gift. She thanked Alderman Sutton for his kind remarks, and asked him to convey to the members of the Council her warmest thanks.

The MAYOR wished to add his humble acknowledg- ments to those already imperfectly given. He had had many tokens of kindness from Warrington, especially from the Town Coungil,and he felt almost overwhelmed by this last evidencetif their esteem and kindness to his daughter. The present wonld serve as a momento of the town of Warrington which, he hoped she would feel dear to her heart as long as she lived. SIR ARCHER CROFT said he desired to thank the Council most sinaerely for their kind present. Alderman FAIRCLOUGH then wished the bride and bildegroom "Very much happiness and a long life," and the deputation withdrew to the billiard room to inspect the presents. The gifts comprised a choice selection of articles in which the silversmiths'.art was shown to advantage.. At the invitation of Mr. and Mrs. Parr, the members of the Council and their wives in- spected the presents on Monday afternoon and were greatlypleased with what they saw.

THE MAYOR, THANKS THE COUNCIL

At the meeting of the Warrington Town Council on Tuesday evening, the IVIAyoR said he wished before the commencement of the business to return grateful thanks to the members of the Council for the handsome present they gave his daughter. (Applause.) As a father he thanked them for their kind interest in his family, and as a friend he thanked them for that additional proof of their kind friendship. He gratefully acknowleged the spontaneous and unexpected gift which, he hoped, he might take it, as a renewed token of their esteem and friendship. . .

Alderman SUTTON ivad the following letter which he had received from Sir Archer Croft :—" Grappenhall Heyes, Warrington. February 2nd, 1903. Dear Mr. Sutton.—Permit rile to write and say on Miss Parr's behalf how sincerely grateful she feels to you and the members of the Warrington Town Council for the very kind compliment you have paid her father and your mayor in recognizing her approaching marriage to me by presenting her with such a beautiful piece of plate. This handsome claret jug will always be to us a most pleasing recollection of a time when Mr. Parr held office in Warrington, and also of the appreciation shown by you all of his good works in the public interest. Please convey to your Council the grateful thanks of Miss Parr and myself for this lovely wedding present.—Believe me, very truly yours, .11. Archer Croft. Mr. Alderman Sutton." PRESENTS FROM THE TENANTRY. •

The tenantry on Mr. Parr's estates at Grappenhall, Antrobus, Whitley and Bruche presented Miss Parr with a massive solid silver waiter. The presentation was made on behalf of the tenants by Mr. Boardman, of Grappenhall, and Mr. Clarke of Whitley. Mr. Bennett, of Brucho was unfortunately unable to attend. The tenants of Mr. R. C. Parr on the Holcroft estate presented Miss Parr with a handsome silver revolving breakfast dish and two entree dishes. This presenta- tion was made on behalf of the tenants, by Mr. Robert Allen and Mr. James Bridge. The tenants of Staunton- on-Arrow presented a silver salver.

THE JUNIOR CONSERVATIVE ASSOCIATION'S

PRESENT.

The present from the committee of the Junior Con- servative Club, Orford-lane, Warrington took the form of a solid silver fruit stand.

• The presentation was made on Monday evening at Grappenhall Hayes, when the association were repre- sented by Messrs. W. L. Williams (chairman), H. Hayes (secretary), James Strettle and H. B. Houghton. Mr. WILLIAMS asked Miss Parr to accept the fruit stand as a small token of the esteem inwhich she is held by the committee of the association. He said he had been desired to offer her the sincerest congratulations on her forthcoming marriage, trusting the future had bright things in store for her, and that she and Sir Archer Croft would enjoy many happy years of married life.

Miss PARR suitably responded, and thanked the mem- bers of the association for their kind gift. Words failed to express her thanks to the members for such a beautiful present. Sir ARCIIER CROFT also thanked the deputation, and expressed the hope that the interest Miss Parr had always shown in the association would he continued.

WAERINGTON LICENSING SESSIONS.

Petition from Local Good Templars.

At the quarterly Session of the Temperance Mission Lodge of the 1.0.G.T.,18r0. C. J. Sheath proposed, and Bro. W. Wakefield seconded that the following petition be sent to the justices at the annual Licensing Sessions :

[COPY.]

To the Worshipflil the 'magistrates of Warring- ton assembled at the annual Licensing Sessions, February 6th; 1903. The memorial of the members of the Temperance Mission Lodge of the Independ- ent Order of .Good Tomplars respectfully sheweth that your memorialists approach your worships with a deep sense of the arduous duties which the legis- lature has imposed upon you for fulfilment of your duties at the annual Licensing Sessions. That your memorialists, in view of the inevitable results °of the traffic in intoxicating liquors in the production of poverty, crime, lunacy, and drunkenneSs, regard the number of licensed houses in the town of War- rington to be excessive, and pray that your wor- ships will endeavour to make reduction in the num- ber of houses, particularly those which have been the most prolific in causing the crime and drunken- ness brought before your worships, for considera- tion and punishment: That your memorialists deeply regret the action taken by the magistrates in the granting of the transfer of the Green Dragon licence from Bridge-street to the "Brooklands," Lovely-lane and pray that your worships will not confirm the transfer. Signed on behalf of the lodge,

F. MERLIN, C.T. C. J. SHEATH, Sec.

INDEPENDENT ORDER OF RECHABITES.

Warrington Consolidated District No. 4.

The annnal meeting of this District was held in the Congregational schoolroom, Ashton, on Saturday after- noon last, and Bro. W. Baxendale, D.C.R., presided over the full complement of District officers and representa- tives. A number of visiting members was also present. The District Secretary reported that the nett numerical, increase for the year was 72, and the financial gain £368 14s. 7d. The membership for adults and juveniles now stand at 1753 and the total funds at £3,727 7s. lOid. The interest earned during the year was equal to 31-1 per cent, on the total capital. During the year property has been built in Wargrave-road, Earlestown, to the value of £2,350 and it is expected that the interest in future will equal 4 per cent., in addition to setting aside a substantial sum per year to form a depreciation account. The Executive has been formed into a Visiting Committee, and nearly all the tents have been seen, with a view to encouraging the members in the work of extension, and the spreading of the principles of total abstinence and thrift. They have also been endeavouring to spread their borders, and increase the - number of tents, a • successful Adult and Juvenile Tent having been Opened at Haydock and it was anticipated that new branches would shortly be opened at Upholland. The reports of the Tent Repre- sentatives were very encouraging and held out the hope of a substantial increase in the strength of the district during 1903. A splendid knife and fork tea was sup- plied by Bro. Clarke and thoroughly enjoyed by all present.

The election of officers for 1903 now took Place and the results were as follows :—District Chief Ruler, Bro. J. Huddleston, Earlestown ; District Denuty Rpler Bro. B. Walker, Latchford; District-Past Chief Ruler, Bro. W. Baxendale, Brynn; District Levite, Bro. W. Antrobus,Golborne ; District Guardian,Bro. R. Harrison, Garswood; District Trustees, Bro. T. Hall, Lymm,l3ro.J. Shaw,Warrington,Bro. 14LWalkden,Brynn ; DistrietSuper- into:idea of Juvenile Tents,Bro. W. Wright.Warrington; District Treasurer, Bro. T. Massey, Warrington ; Dis- trict Secretary, Bro.- T. Winstanley, Ashton; District Auditors, Bro. 13. Warburton' Warrington and Bro. B. Clarke, A'shton: Agent for Wigan property, Bro. T. Winstanley, Ashton; Agent for Earlestown property, Bro. S. Pellowe, Earlestown. •

Bro. Winstanley, Ashton, and Bro Wright, Warrington; were appointed representatives to attend the High Mov- hie Conference to be held at Swansea, in August nest.

EARLESTOWN ORCHESTRAL SOCIETY.

The annual concert promoted by this Society took Place in the Town Hall,EarlestownonThursday evening. There was a large and appreciative audience, and an excellent programme was submitted. The selections by the band, which was conducted by Mr. A. Ashley, com- prised " Tannhauser," " "The Jolly Coppersmith," "Ethiopian" musical sketch "Plantation holiday," and descriptive galop "Post Horn," each of which was well rendered. Other instrumental items were, flute solo "Fantaisie from Chopin" and piccolo solo "Scotch airs," by Mr.. J. F. Ridgway. The vocal selections were song (a) "Where'er you walk," (b) "Droop not, young lover," "The Bedouin love song," and " Thou'rt passing hence," by Mr. Arthur %%Yardley (bass), which were well received. The humorous business was in the hands of Mr. Frank Crawford, and his representations evoked much amusement. Mr. E. Latimer (formerly of Endes- town) ably presided at the piano.

WARRINGTON AND THE SHIP CANAL COMPANY.

THE DREDGING OF THE MERSEY. Mr. Hunter Gross-Examined.

In the Chancery Division on Monday, Mr. Justice Byrne resumed the hearing of the action brought by J. Crosfield and Sons' and, other Warrington traders against the Manchester Ship Canal Company claiming rend in respect of the alleged failure of the Canal Company to dredge the river Mersey in accordance with its Parliamentary obligations. This was the fifteenth day of the trial.

Mr. William Henry Hunter, the chief engineer of the Ship Cam,' Company, in further examination, said he had appealed to the Warrington Corporation officials again and again to do their duty in the matter of the lime in the river,,but his appeals had been ineffectual. In cross-examination by Mr. Bousfleld, K.C., Mr. Hunter said he adhered to the opinion that Mr. Long- din was satisfied with his explanation that the employ- ment of .more dredgers was practically useless, and said that as he was constantly seeing that gentleman,he ought to have told him if he was dissatisfied, and not have written one letter only, expressing dissatisfaction with the way in which the work was being carried out. Mr. Longdin had taken a horizontal instead of a sloping datum, the importance of which was shown by the fact that while the low water of spring tides at Warrington bridge was 10ft.,at Bank Quay it was 6in. or Bin. lower. In the dredger reports it was stated that in some sec- tions the dredger had left a narrow channel of 7ft. of water behind it, and it was put to witness that that was a clear admission that the statutory depth of Bft.had not been dredged. The witness replied that 71t. might have been dredged An the first instance, or there might have been a mistake in the entry. Messrs. Crosfield had I changed their plant, at an expense of some £5,000, with the effect that less lime flowed into the river. He had found lime in the Mersey in quantities which, in his opinion, would have justified a further prosecution of Messrs. Crosfield. The Mersey Conservancy Board did not prosecute because of the difficulty of identifying the particular works from which the lime came. That difficulty could be surmounted by .the Cor- poration, who alone had power to examine the sewers. In one of the dredger reports of 1897 it was stated that Crosfield's boats were grounding almost daily near the spelter works, and the dredger could not be expected to get there for five weeks. The witness was asked whether it was tolerable that such a state of blockage should be allowed to exist. He replied that in the agreement, provision was made for obstructions occurring in the channel, .and he must have time to re- move them. They could not be removed by magic. Mr. Justice Byrne said it appeared to him that the Canal Company could get rid of a great many more barge loads if they had two dredgers at work. The witness said that was exactly what he had tried to do, but he found as a matter of practical working that they did not get more output from two dredgers than from one. The difficulty was in keeping up the tipping roads, which were made from the dredgings and which contained more or less fluid material. The case was not as if they were dealing with solid earth. What- ever steps were taken, there was no possibility of dredging the Six-acre Reach and Monks, Hall, and Co.'s works at the same time.

Mr. Justice Byrne said it might be that before he had done with the case he should have to ask for a report from some perfectly independent engineer. In furth6r cross-examination the witness said that in August, 1899, the Canal Company received notice of obstructions in the channel near Monks, Hall, and Co.'s works. It was five months before that obstruction was dealt with, but under the circumstances it was impos- sittle to do the work quicker. Many of the boats which grounded were outside the channel at the time, and the majority of those that grounded in the channel did so because they were overloaded. Several of the witnesses for the plaintiffs had said that since the opening of the canal the water level had fallen generally from one foot to eighteen inches, but he denied that that was the case except for a short period after the opening of the canal. The hearing was adjourned.

In the Chancery Division on Tuesday Mr. Justice Byrne resumed the hearing of the action brought by Messrs. J. Crositeld and Sons and other traders of War- rington against the Manchester Ship Canal Company in respect of the alleged failure of the latter to dredge the river Mersey at Warrington sufficiently. Mr. W. H. Hunter, chief engineer of the Ship Canal, further cross-examined, admitted that the dredging report of August. 1894, at low water marks, showed that the Company had made a channel 60 feet wide and 10 feet deep, and that the extra depth was allowed for silting up. That was not an admission on the part of the company to make an allowance for silting. The case for the defence was then closed. REBUTTING EVIDENCE.

The judge allowed the plaintiffs to call rebutting evidence with respect to the pouring of lime from the plaintiffs' work into the river. Mr. James Newall, chief engineer for Messrs. J. Crosfield and Sons, denied that since 1898 there had been any possibility of lime getting into the river from the firm's drains,except in infinitesimal quantities from the en tch-pi ts.

Dr. Markel, chemist and managing director of Messrs., Crosfield's works, said the connection by which lime used to get into the river had been cut. It would cause a loss of some thousands of pounds if they were to re- vert to the old practice of putting lime into the river. He did not know whether lime came down the Corporation .sewers from the works of other manu- facturers.

Mr. Cripps, K.C., summed up the case for the Canal Company, contending that they had discharged their obligations. Further, their obligations were contractual and not being parties to the agreement of 1896 the traders had no right to bring an action of this kind. He ridiculed the contention of theplaintiffs that the Canal Companyshould construct and dredge a channel so that at all time there should be a depth of Bft. of water, irrespective of the state of the tides, pointing out that such a construction of the agreement gave no effect to the important words "at low water of spring tides." The whole basis of the case was whether the 'Canal Company's obligation was in reference to a fixed datum or not. He submitted that it was. With regard to the right of the traders to free navigation, that was only to run at such times as the Canal Company were not carrying out the dredging to the reasonable satisfaction of the borough surveyor of Warrington. Until this action arose,no dissatisfac- tion was ever expressed to the Ship Canal Company by Mr. Longdin ; and he said under these circumstances the plaintiffs' were stopped from claiming damages or the return of tolls paid. Mr. Justice Byrne said the plaintiffs' case was that penalties attached from the time, of the notices that the Ship Canal Company had not complied with its statutory obligations. Mr. Cripps said the evidence was that when notices were given,the Ship Canal Company ationce sent their dredger, and continued to dredge until the obstruction was cleared away. Counsel had not concluded his speech when the hear- ing was adjourned.

Mr. Cripps. K.C., resuming his slimming up on behalf of the defendant company by citing authorities forthe proposition that an agreement between parties, which was inserted in an Act of Parliament, was still a con- tractual agreement between the parties who had entered into it, under which none but the parties could sue. Dealing with the question of lime in the river, which was of considerable importance, the learned counsel then proceeded to review and comment on the evid- ence. The hearing was adjourned.

On Thursday Pr. Cripps, K;C.. continued his slimming up of the defendant's case. On the evidence he con- tended that the dredging plant was sufficient to remove all the obstructions in the river which the Canal Com- pany were liable to clear away. The mandatory injunc• tion asked for by the plaintiffs was one, he submitted, which it was impossible for the Court to grant. Mr. Justice Byrne said he did not understand that a mandatory injunction was asked for at the bar, but the order asked for by the plaintiffs would have to be formulated.

Mr. 13ousileld. replying on the whole case for the plain- tiffs, contended that they had a right to sue for relief under the Act ot 1885 and the agreement of 1896 and that they had done nothing to sacrifice it. The damages would be in the nature of a penalty because the Canal Company had not fulfilled its statutory obligations. The hearing was again adjourned.

Death. PEARSE—On the 23th ult. at Varnton Vicarage in Oxford, Mary Emma; widow of the late Percival Pearse, aged 63 years, after a short illness of pneumonia, was interred Ya rnton Churchyard January 28th. WATK NS.—January 30th, at Kirkby, Newton-le-Willows, suddenly, John Webb Watkins, M.D., aged 69. • in Memoriam. In loving remembrance of Elizabeth (Bessie) wife,of Thomas Rowland, who departed this life January 28th, 1892. "We shall meet again."
